   |                                           /
   |   LUNI: seasonal, flows SW to the        /
   |   Rann of Kachchh                       /
   |                                        /
   |         fertile patches in the Bagar  /
   |         are called "ROHI"            /
   |                                     /
   +------------------------------------+--------------------------->
       |                                |
       |          25 cm ISOHYET  -------+  <-- the BAGAR's WESTERN
       |          (Khullar's own boundary)    limit; the Bagar runs
       |                                      NE-SW from the Aravalli
       |                                      edge to this line
       |
   SIX DUNE TYPES mapped from LANDSAT across this transect:
   obstacle | parabolic | longitudinal | transverse | barchan |
   shrub-coppice   -> L6's quadrant, read along a single traverse

   THE READING: FOUR things change together west to east - elevation
   RISES, rainfall RISES, dune height FALLS, and vegetation INCREASES.
   No single statement about "the Thar" can be true along the whole line.
```

One traverse, six named local terms, four gradients. The scale-control clause the Basic owner writes is cashed here, and it is the reason this is a heavy lesson rather than a paragraph.

### 1. Why subdivision is an analytical act and not a vocabulary exercise

⚠️ The Basic owner's scale-control clause does not say that the Thar is varied. It says something much stronger: that the **western dune fields, the eastern Aravalli transition, the Luni-playa system and the canal-command tracts cannot be represented by one uniform desert condition**. ❗ That is a prohibition on a type of sentence, and it has three immediate consequences for answer writing.

1. ❗ **No degradation statement about "the Thar" is meaningful without a subregion.** Wind erosion dominates in one part, salinity in another, and vegetation degradation in a third.
2. ❗ **No restoration measure is correct for "the Thar".** Lesson 16's whole argument is that a measure must be matched to a pathway, and the pathway varies along this transect.
3. ❗ **No area figure is meaningful without stating which subregions it includes** — which is Lesson 10's four-figure spread, now given a physiographic cause.

### 2. The regionalisation, and why Khullar bothers to give its history

✅ Khullar, at folios 90 to 91, sets his own subdivision inside a history of attempts, and naming that history is worth a mark because it shows that a regional boundary is a **scholarly decision**, not a natural fact:

| Scholar | Date and work | Scheme |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **L.D. Stamp** | **1922** | *"the first scholar who made an attempt to classify natural regions or physiographic regions of India"*; the scheme was based on *"the homogeneity of physiography, structure and climate."* He divided India into **3 major natural regions and 22 sub-regions** |
| ✅ **J.N.L. Baker** | **1928** | *"Another attempt was made by J.N.L. Baker in 1928. His scheme was based on the work of Wood and was in close agreement with the approach adopted by Stamp and Baker"* |
| ✅ **O.H.K. Spate** | **1954**, revised in the third edition of *India and Pakistan: A General and Regional Geography*, **1967** | **3 macro regions**, further divided into **34 regions of first order (excluding islands)**, **74 regions of second order** and **225 subdivisions**. ✅ The three macro regions *"as suggested by Spate are (i) the mountain rim, (ii) the Indo-Gangetic plain and (iii) the Peninsula"* |
| ✅ **S.P. Chatterjee** | **1965**, in *The Gazetteer of India*, Volume I | **Seven major physiographic divisions on the basis of topography**: Northern Mountains, Great Plains, Central Highlands, Peninsular Plateaus, East Coast, West Coast, and Bordering Seas and Islands |

⚠️ **The point of the table is the spread**: three regions, or seven, or two hundred and twenty-five, depending on the criterion and the resolution. ❗ **A region is an answer to a question, and different questions produce different regions** — which is the same methodological finding as Lesson 10's area spread and Lesson 14's figure contradiction, arriving from a third direction.

✅ Khullar's own placement puts the Thar inside **Fig. 3.16 "Physiographic Divisions"**, where the relevant unit is labelled **WESTERN PLAINS**, and his folio 92 paragraph is the compact statement of the whole transect: *"The Western plain consists of arid land of Rajasthan. It has a well defined boundary in the east marked by the Aravalli range. This is a sandy desert with distinct aeolian topography. The eastern part is less sandy, comparatively more humid and is clothed with steppe vegetation."*

❗ Note the three claims packed into four sentences: an **eastern boundary**, a **dominant process** — *"distinct aeolian topography"* — and an **internal gradient** in sandiness, humidity and vegetation. ⚠️ Everything below elaborates those three.

### 3. The transect, west to east, with the sources' own terms

✅ **Overall frame (Khullar, folio 76).** The Thar is *"about 650 km long and 250-300 km wide"*, *"an undulating plain whose average elevation is about 325 m above mean sea level,"* which *"descends to about 150 m above mean sea level near the Indo-Pak border as well as towards the Indus Valley and the Rann of Kachchh."*

❗ **Read the elevation correctly, because it is counter-intuitive.** ⚠️ The land does **not** rise from the border to the Aravalli in a simple ramp; the figure of 325 metres is an **average for the whole desert**, and the low ground at about **150 metres** lies at the **western and south-western edges** — toward the Indus Valley and the Rann. ⚠️ The Marusthali itself averages **200 to 250 metres**. ✅ East of the range, the **East Rajasthan Upland** rises to **250 to 500 metres** (Khullar, folio 93). ❗ So the overall tendency along the traverse is **low in the west and south-west, higher in the east**, with the Aravalli as a ridge between two plains.

⚠️ **Unit 1 — the Marusthali, the desert proper.** ✅ *"The desert proper is called Marusthali and accounts for greater part of the Marwar plain. The average elevation of the Marusthali is 200-250 m above sea level."* ✅ It is internally split: *"In general, the eastern part of the Marusthali is rocky while its western part is covered by shifting sand dunes locally known as dhrian."*

❗ **Dhrian is the term to carry**, and it carries a process: ⚠️ *shifting* dunes are **live dunes** in Lesson 6's sense — unvegetated and mobile — as distinct from fixed dunes rooted with vegetation. 🔁 **That is a direct back-reference to Lesson 6**, and it means the western Marusthali is where dune mobility is a present process rather than an inherited form.

✅ **The structural finding that underlies the eastern Marusthali's rockiness** — 🔁 already used at Lessons 2 and 10 — is worth restating here because it is the physiographic key: the desert *"has a vast stretch of sand with a few outcrops of bedrock of gneisses, schists and granites which proves that geologically it is a part of the Peninsular Plateau and it is only at the surface that it looks like an aggradational plain."*

⚠️ **Unit 2 — the thali.** ✅ *"The tract north of the Luni is known as thali or sandy plain."* ⚠️ A short entry in the source and a useful one in an answer, because it names a **sandy tract defined by its position relative to a drainage line** rather than by its surface alone.

⚠️ **Unit 3 — the Rajasthan Bagar, the semi-arid belt.** ✅ *"The eastern part of the Thar Desert, upto the Aravali Range is a semi-arid plain which is known as the Rajasthan Bagar. It runs in a north-east to south-west direction from the edge of the Aravali in the east to the 25 cm isohyet in the west. It is drained by a number of short seasonal streams originating from the Aravali and supports agriculture in some patches of fertile tracts called rohi."*

❗ **Four facts in one sentence, and all four are examinable.** ⚠️ The Bagar is **semi-arid, not arid**. Its **western limit is a climatic line — the 25 centimetre isohyet** — while its **eastern limit is a structural one, the Aravalli edge**, so the unit is defined by two different kinds of boundary at its two ends. It is **drained by short seasonal streams from the Aravalli**, which makes it the one part of the Thar with a reliable external water supply. And it **supports agriculture in fertile patches called rohi** — ❗ which means the Bagar is where **people and the arid system interact most intensively**, and therefore where land degradation has the most at stake.

⚠️ **Unit 4 — the Aravalli edge.** ✅ Khullar, folio 93: *"The Aravalli range runs for a distance of 800 km from Delhi to Ahmedabad."* ✅ The repository map bank gives the axis as **south-west to north-east** and flags the confusion to avoid: the Aravalli *"Runs SW-NE, unlike the Vindhya-Satpura east-west tendency."* ⚠️ ❗ The range is treated in this lesson strictly as a **boundary and a transition**; the *"desert barrier"* framing is examined and qualified at Lesson 13 and Lesson 17.

### 4. The dune evidence, read along the transect

✅ Khullar's LANDSAT classification, 🔁 introduced at Lesson 6, is best read as a traverse rather than as a list: **obstacle, parabolic, longitudinal, transverse, barchan and shrub-coppice**.

⚠️ 🔁 **Lesson 6's quadrant turns this into an argument.** ❗ **Barchan and transverse together** mean the supply axis is sampled at both ends within one desert. ❗ **Parabolic, obstacle and shrub-coppice together** mean **vegetation is present and actively interacting with sand across much of the area** — a shrub-coppice dune is a mound accumulated around a living shrub, and an obstacle dune is sand trapped behind an obstruction. ❗ **Longitudinal** confirms a sustained resultant transport direction, corroborated independently by ✅ Leong, who names *"the Thar Desert"* among the world's extensive seif provinces.

✅ **The dimensional gradient is the strongest single piece of Indian evidence in this lesson:** *"The mean height of the dunes varies from 8 m in Jaipur and Sikar districts to 30 m in Barmer district. The length of the dunes also varies greatly and some may be 5-10 km long."*

⚠️ **Read west to east: 30 metres at Barmer in the arid west, 8 metres near Jaipur and Sikar on the semi-arid east.** ❗ That is a **near-fourfold fall in dune height across the transect**, and it is consistent with everything else on the line — sand supply falls eastward, rainfall rises eastward, vegetation increases eastward, and dunes correspondingly shrink and become vegetation-associated rather than free.

❗ **The limitation of this evidence, stated.** ⚠️ Khullar gives **no date for the imagery, no area per dune type and no mobility status**, so the inventory describes **form**, not **activity**. 🔁 Lesson 6 flagged this; it matters again here because a transect of forms is **not** a transect of degradation rates, and Lesson 14 will show how easily the two are conflated.

### 5. The eight attributes, used as structure rather than reproduced

⚠️ The advanced owner's §2 table carries eight attribute rows. ❗ Rather than reproducing it, this edition **uses it as the checklist a Thar answer must satisfy**, with each row answered from the sources above:

| Attribute | How the transect answers it |
|---|---|
| ✅ **Location** | West of the Aravalli, between the Indus and the Aravalli, spilling into Punjab, Haryana and northern Gujarat |
| ✅ **Area** | ❗ Boundary-dependent; four figures, four pages, no single value (🔁 Lesson 10) |
| ✅ **Rainfall** | Under 50 cm annually; under 25 cm in the arid core; the **25 cm isohyet** is a working internal boundary. 🔁 Regime routed to Topic 18 |
| ✅ **Rann relationship** | ❗ Geomorphically a **saline mudflat and marsh system**, distinct from the sandy Thar — the correction developed at Lesson 12 |
| ✅ **Aeolian core** | **Jaisalmer and Barmer**, where the advanced owner places *"classic aeolian topography dominated by sand dunes"*, corroborated by the 30-metre dune heights at Barmer |
| ✅ **Economy** | **Bajra, pulses, fodder; livestock central**; date-palm, water-melon and rainwater-harvesting scope. 🔁 Agricultural systems belong to the agriculture owner; named once, not developed |
| ✅ **Degradation driver** | Wind erosion, unsuitable cultivation, poor moisture conservation, overgrazing — developed at Lessons 14 and 16 |
| ✅ **Conservation direction** | Agroforestry, pasture management, water conservation, reclamation — developed at Lesson 16 |

### 6. 🇮🇳 India example and its limitation — the mosaic as the Indian case itself

⚠️ This lesson's India example **is** the lesson, so its limitation must be stated more carefully than usual.

❗ **Limitation one: the transect is a generalisation of a generalisation.** ⚠️ Khullar's units are **physiographic regions defined for a textbook**, not mapped boundaries with coordinates. ❗ The 25 centimetre isohyet in particular is a **climatic average line that moves between years** — and at a monsoon margin (🔁 Lesson 10) it moves a great deal. ⚠️ A boundary drawn on a fluctuating isohyet is a boundary that fluctuates.

❗ **Limitation two: the vocabulary is regional and is not standardised.** ⚠️ *Marusthali*, *dhrian*, *bagar*, *rohi* and *thali* are **local terms recorded by one author**; other sources use other terms or none. ❗ Use them **with the source attached**, exactly as with the area figures.

❗ **Limitation three: the canal command is missing from this picture entirely.** ⚠️ Khullar's physiographic transect is a **pre-irrigation description of surface form**. The Indira Gandhi Canal command — ✅ the canal that Majid Husain's India extremes list records as **682 kilometres** long — overlies part of the western Thar and has changed land use, water table and salinity there. ❗ **The Basic owner's scale-control clause names canal-command tracts as a separate unit for exactly this reason**, and this edition adds it at Lesson 16 rather than pretending the physiographic transect already contains it.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

**The comparison that matters is Marusthali against Bagar, because almost every Thar statement is true of one and false of the other.**

| | Marusthali (desert proper) | Rajasthan Bagar (semi-arid belt) |
|---|---|---|
| Climatic class | **Arid** | **Semi-arid** |
| Rainfall | Below the 25 cm isohyet | Between the 25 cm isohyet and the Aravalli |
| Surface | ✅ Shifting dunes (**dhrian**) in the west; **rocky** in the east | ⚠️ *"Less sandy, comparatively more humid"*, ✅ *"clothed with steppe vegetation"* |
| Elevation | ✅ **200-250 m** | Rising toward the Aravalli; the upland beyond reaches **250-500 m** |
| Drainage | Largely internal; the Luni crosses it to the Rann | ✅ *"Short seasonal streams originating from the Aravali"* |
| Agriculture | Very limited; livestock-dominated | ✅ Supported *"in some patches of fertile tracts called rohi"* |
| Dune height | ✅ Up to a mean of **30 m** at Barmer | ✅ About **8 m** near Jaipur and Sikar |
| Dominant degradation concern | ⚠️ **Dune mobility and deflation** | ⚠️ **Cultivation pressure, cover loss and water erosion on a cultivated semi-arid surface** |

**Strongest objection.** Local vocabulary is regional-geography trivia. An examiner asking about aeolian processes does not want five Rajasthani terms.

**Reply.** The terms are not the deliverable; **the gradients they name are**. ❗ Four quantities change together along this line — elevation, rainfall, dune height and vegetation — and that co-variation is what licenses the single most valuable sentence available about the Indian arid zone: *"Degradation in the Thar is not one process but a west-to-east sequence of processes, so a measure appropriate to a shifting dune at Barmer is inappropriate to a cultivated semi-arid tract near Sikar."* ⚠️ That sentence cannot be written without the mosaic. ❗ And one term, **dhrian**, is doing real analytical work: it names **shifting** dunes specifically, which is the live-against-fixed distinction of Lesson 6 recorded in local usage.

⚠️ **Residual.** The **position of the internal boundaries is not fixed** by any source available to this edition. ❗ No coordinates, no areas for the Marusthali, the thali or the Bagar, and no mapped line for the 25 centimetre isohyet are given. ⚠️ The transect is therefore a **sequence**, reliable in its order and its gradients, and not a map with measurable widths. ❗ This file states the order and declines to state the widths.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** *"Geographical features and their location-changes"* and the physical-geography clause, General Studies Paper I; the subject master framework's arid-geomorphology line applied to India.
- **Verified linkage:** ✅ **2026 General Studies Paper I, Question 6.** This lesson supplies the **spatial differentiation** that the owned answer's qualification needs — aeolian processes dominate in one part of the arid zone, not uniformly across it. ⚠️ It also supports the mapwork at Lesson 17, where every feature named here is placed.
- **Traps:** writing "the Thar" as a uniform object; placing the Bagar west of the Marusthali; treating the 25 centimetre isohyet as a fixed line; reading the 325-metre average as the elevation at the Aravalli foot; using local terms without the source; assuming the dune inventory indicates activity rather than form.
- **Answer use:** the mosaic sentence is the one to carry into any Thar answer. *"The Thar is a west-to-east sequence rather than a uniform surface: shifting dunes locally called dhrian on the western Marusthali at mean heights up to thirty metres near Barmer, a rocky eastern Marusthali on Peninsular bedrock, the thali north of the Luni, and beyond the twenty-five centimetre isohyet the semi-arid Rajasthan Bagar with its cultivated rohi patches, closing on the Aravalli."* ❗ That one sentence demonstrates the scale control the Basic owner demands.

Revision notes:

- ❗ **The scale-control clause is the warrant:** western dune fields, eastern Aravalli transition, Luni-playa system and canal-command tracts **cannot be one uniform desert condition**.
- ✅ **Regionalisation history (Khullar, folios 90-91):** **L.D. Stamp 1922** — 3 major natural regions, 22 sub-regions; **J.N.L. Baker 1928**; **O.H.K. Spate 1954**, revised **1967** — 3 macro regions, **34 first-order (excluding islands), 74 second-order, 225 subdivisions**; macro regions *"(i) the mountain rim, (ii) the Indo-Gangetic plain and (iii) the Peninsula"*; **S.P. Chatterjee 1965**, *Gazetteer of India* Vol. I — **seven divisions**.
- ⚠️ **The lesson of that history:** three regions or seven or 225, depending on criterion and resolution. **A region is an answer to a question.**
- ✅ **Khullar folio 92:** *"The Western plain consists of arid land of Rajasthan. It has a well defined boundary in the east marked by the Aravalli range. This is a sandy desert with distinct aeolian topography. The eastern part is less sandy, comparatively more humid and is clothed with steppe vegetation."*
- ✅ **Dimensions and elevations:** **650 km long, 250-300 km wide**; overall average **~325 m**; descending to **~150 m** near the Indo-Pak border and toward the Indus Valley and the Rann; **Marusthali 200-250 m**; East Rajasthan Upland beyond the range **250-500 m**.
- ✅ **Marusthali** = the desert proper, greater part of the **Marwar plain**; **eastern part rocky** (gneiss, schist, granite outcrops → **Peninsular Plateau** beneath), **western part shifting dunes locally called dhrian**.
- ✅ **Thali** = *"the tract north of the Luni"*, a sandy plain.
- ✅ **Rajasthan Bagar** = semi-arid plain from the **Aravalli edge** east to the **25 cm isohyet** west, running **NE-SW**, drained by *"short seasonal streams originating from the Aravali"*, with fertile patches called **rohi**.
- ❗ **The Bagar has a structural eastern boundary and a climatic western one** — two different kinds of line on one unit.
- ✅ **Aravalli:** *"runs for a distance of 800 km from Delhi to Ahmedabad"*; map bank axis **SW-NE**, ❗ unlike the Vindhya-Satpura east-west tendency.
- ✅ **Six LANDSAT dune types:** obstacle · parabolic · longitudinal · transverse · barchan · shrub-coppice. 🔁 Read through **L6's quadrant**: both supply ends sampled; **vegetation-associated forms abundant**; ✅ Leong names the Thar among seif provinces.
- ✅ **Dune gradient:** mean height **30 m at Barmer** → **8 m at Jaipur and Sikar**; lengths **5-10 km**. ⚠️ Near-fourfold fall west to east.
- ❗ **Four gradients move together west to east:** elevation up, rainfall up, dune height down, vegetation up.
- ❗ **Limitations:** units are **textbook regions**, not mapped boundaries; the **25 cm isohyet moves between years**; the vocabulary is **one author's**; the **canal command is absent** from this physiographic picture and is added at L16. ✅ Majid records the **Indira Gandhi Canal at 682 km**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A state agency proposes a single uniform dune-stabilisation programme — mechanical sand barriers followed by tree planting — to be applied at identical specification across the whole of western Rajasthan. Using the Thar's internal mosaic, explain why a uniform specification is likely to be inefficient, and identify where it would be best and worst matched.

**Model answer:** A uniform specification fails because four properties change together along the west-to-east transect, so the problem the programme addresses is not the same problem at both ends. In the **western Marusthali**, around Barmer and Jaisalmer, mean dune heights reach about thirty metres, the dunes are shifting — recorded locally as **dhrian** — and rainfall is below the twenty-five centimetre isohyet. Here the dominant process genuinely is **sand mobility**, so mechanical barriers that break the saltation cascade and anchor a surface are well matched; but the same aridity that makes the problem acute also makes the tree-planting component fragile, since establishment under twenty-five centimetres of highly variable rainfall is difficult and failure is likely without protection and water. In the **eastern Marusthali**, the surface is rocky, with gneiss, schist and granite outcrops on Peninsular bedrock, and there is comparatively little free sand to stabilise; a dune programme there addresses a problem of small extent. In the **Rajasthan Bagar**, east of the isohyet and up to the Aravalli, the land is semi-arid, less sandy, more humid, clothed with steppe vegetation and cultivated in fertile **rohi** patches drained by short seasonal streams from the range; mean dune heights fall to about eight metres near Jaipur and Sikar. Here the operative degradation pathways are **cultivation pressure, loss of cover and water erosion on a worked semi-arid surface**, so sand barriers are largely beside the point while cover management, pasture and field-level practices are not. The programme is therefore best matched in the west, redundant in the rocky east of the Marusthali, and mismatched in the Bagar — and the physiographic reason is that no single statement about "the Thar" holds along the whole line.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the Rajasthan Bagar as simply "a less severe Marusthali". It is not the same system at lower intensity; it has a different water supply from Aravalli seasonal streams, a different land use with cultivated rohi tracts, a different vegetation cover and therefore a different dominant degradation pathway.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "The Thar cannot be treated as a single surface." Discuss with reference to its internal physiography. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The claim is sound, and a west-to-east traverse establishes it. The desert proper, the Marusthali, averages two hundred to two hundred and fifty metres, with shifting dunes locally called dhrian in its west and a rocky eastern part whose gneiss, schist and granite outcrops show it rests on the Peninsular Plateau. North of the seasonal Luni lies the sandy thali. Beyond the twenty-five centimetre isohyet the semi-arid Rajasthan Bagar runs north-east to south-west up to the Aravalli, less sandy, more humid, clothed in steppe vegetation and cultivated in fertile patches called rohi, drained by short seasonal streams from the range. Khullar's dune measurements confirm the gradient: mean heights fall from about thirty metres at Barmer to about eight metres near Jaipur and Sikar. Elevation, rainfall and vegetation rise eastward while dune height falls. The qualification is that these are textbook regions, and the isohyet boundary itself shifts between years.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the named subdivisions placed in correct west-to-east order with their local terms; 4 for at least three co-varying gradients identified with sourced quantities attached; 2 for the differing dominant degradation pathway drawn as the consequence; 1 for the qualification that the units are textbook regions on a shifting climatic boundary. Deduct 2 for placing the Bagar west of the Marusthali.

## Lesson 12 — Inland drainage and salt: the Luni, the playa lakes, and the Rann

Progress: L12 / 18 — Inland drainage and salt: the Luni, the playa lakes, and the Rann  |  Stage: Applied  |  Band: Standard

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Canonical basis: Advanced owner §1 and §5 — the **Rann of Kachchh correction** in its own words: older regional descriptions *"link it with the Rann of Kachchh, but the Rann is a distinct saline mudflat/marsh system, not simply another field of Thar dunes"*, with the §5 trap recording that Khullar *"treats the Rann of Kachchh as an EXTENSION of the Thar desert"*; §2 attribute table, Rann row — *"Arid-region linkage, but geomorphically a saline mudflat/marsh distinct from sandy Thar."* Basic owner §8.3, playa and salt pan as *"ephemeral lake floor in an interior basin; evaporation concentrates salts"*; the terminology-control clause separating **playa from the wider desert**.
Book basis: D.R. Khullar, local scan PDF p.55, folios 76 to 77, for the Luni, the inland-drainage area, the named saline lakes and the Sambhar measurements; local scan PDF p.121 and p.122, folios 208 to 211, for India's salinity extent and **Table 7.2 "Salinity Affected Areas in India"**, with **Fig. 7.4 "India: Saline and Alkaline Lands"**. G.C. Leong, local scan PDF p.64, for the closed-basin salt mechanism and the playa, salina, salar and shott vocabulary.
CA: none obtained for this mechanism. The Thar's inland drainage and salt system is a book-physiography question for which no first-party government or multilateral document was fetched this pass; the national salinity anchors used at Lessons 14 to 16 bear on degradation extent, not on drainage configuration.
Boundary: 🔁 **Topic 09 owns lake classification and lake death**; this lesson treats playa and saline-lake formation strictly as a **drainage and salt-budget outcome** and classifies no lake. 🔁 **Topic 34** owns the 2022 objective demand on a West African lake drying into desert; it is named once as an analogue and not taught. 🔁 Topic 09 also owns the 2026 objective demand on a desert lake and its heritage status; named once as a boundary. ❗ **No answer letter, elimination cue or key is recorded or inferred for either.** 🔁 Topic 04 owns groundwater and capillary mechanics.
Trap: treating the Rann as "more Thar" and the saline lakes as ordinary lakes that happen to be salty. Both errors come from the same failure to think in terms of a **budget**.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

   +----------------+           ^                     |
        every input              |                    |
        carries SOLUTES          +--- vapour carries  |
                                      NO solutes <----+

   THEREFORE:  d(salt)/dt  >  0,  ALWAYS, while the basin is closed.
   The lake's AREA oscillates with the season; its SALT only accumulates.
   Sambhar: "about 225 sq km during the rainy season but shrinks
   considerably in dry season" - AREA varies, MASS OF SALT does not fall.
```

Two panels. The first is a map you could draw; the second is an accounting identity you cannot escape. Together they explain every salt feature in the Indian arid zone.

### 1. The question, in plain terms

⚠️ Water falls on western Rajasthan, runs off the Aravalli, and gathers in low ground. ❗ **Where does it go?** In most of India the answer is "to the sea, via a river". ⚠️ In this region, for a large area, the answer is **nowhere**: ✅ Khullar records *"a large area of inland drainage on the eastern edge of the Thar Desert having several saline lakes."*

❗ 🔁 **Lesson 8 established the consequence in general form** — in a closed basin, water leaves only as vapour and vapour carries no solutes, so salt accumulates by arithmetic rather than by chemistry. ⚠️ This lesson applies that identity to a named Indian system, and then uses it to correct a specific error about the Rann.

### 2. The network, as the source describes it

✅ **The through-flowing element.** *"Even the important river, the Luni, is a seasonal stream which flows towards the south-west to the Rann of Kachchh."* ⚠️ The Luni is the Thar's principal drainage line, and three features of it are examinable: it is **seasonal**, not perennial; it flows **south-west**, not toward the Ganga system; and it terminates in the **Rann**, not in the Arabian Sea. ✅ The repository map bank states the terminal fact as a trap line: the Luni *"Does not normally reach the Arabian Sea as a perennial river."*

✅ **The feeder element.** The Rajasthan Bagar *"is drained by a number of short seasonal streams originating from the Aravali."* ⚠️ 🔁 Lesson 11 noted that this is the Thar's one reliable external water supply; here it is also the **principal solute supply**, because water running off a weathering rock mass carries dissolved weathering products with it.

✅ **The closed element.** *"North of the Luni basin, there is a large area of inland drainage on the eastern edge of the Thar Desert having several saline lakes. They are a great source of common salt and many other salts. The Sambhar, the Didwana, the Degana, the Kuchaman, the Sargol and the Khatu are some of the important lakes."*

✅ **The type case, with measurements.** *"The largest and the most outstanding is the Sambhar lake about 65 km west of Jaipur. Situated at an altitude of about 360 m in the Aravali terrain, this lake is about 30 km long with an average width of 3-8 km. It occupies an area of about 225 sq km during the rainy season but shrinks considerably in dry season."*

⚠️ **Three things in that passage are worth isolating.** ❗ The lakes are *"a great source of common salt"* — the salt is of **commercial grade and quantity**, which is direct evidence that the budget has been running a surplus for a very long time. ❗ Sambhar sits *"in the Aravali terrain"* at about **360 metres**, which places the inland-drainage area on the **eastern, higher side of the desert** rather than in its arid core. ❗ And the area *"shrinks considerably in dry season"* — ⚠️ **this is a playa in all but name**, and it is the cleanest Indian instance of the ephemeral-lake behaviour described in the world material.

### 3. The budget, stated once and applied three times

⚠️ The identity in Panel B is worth writing out because it does three jobs in this file.

1. ⚠️ **Every input carries solutes.** Rainfall carries dissolved atmospheric constituents; runoff carries weathering products from the Aravalli; groundwater carries whatever it has dissolved along its path, including salts from older deposits.
2. ⚠️ **Evaporation removes water and nothing else.** Water vapour is solute-free.
3. ⚠️ **There is no outflow.** The basin is closed.
4. ❗ **Therefore the mass of salt in the basin can only rise.** The lake's **area** oscillates with the season — the summer monsoon fills it, the dry season shrinks it — but the **salt does not leave**, so each cycle concentrates it further and precipitates a crust when the water retreats.

❗ **Application one, in this lesson:** it explains why Rajasthan's inland lakes are commercially saline rather than merely brackish.

❗ **Application two, at Lesson 14:** it explains why **Rajasthan appears in India's salinity table at all**, in a list otherwise dominated by canal-irrigated and coastal states. ✅ Khullar's **Table 7.2 "Salinity Affected Areas in India"**, at folio 210, gives Rajasthan **7.28 lakh hectares**, in a table led by Uttar Pradesh at **12.95**, Punjab at **12.25** and Gujarat at **12.14 lakh hectares**. ⚠️ Rajasthan's salinity has a **natural accumulation component** that a canal-irrigated plain's does not.

❗ **Application three, at Lesson 16:** it explains why irrigation in a formerly closed arid basin is **dangerous in a specific way**. ⚠️ 🔁 Lesson 9 made the mechanism available: the salt is **already present**, stored in the profile and the shallow groundwater. ❗ Raising the water table does not import salt; it **relocates stored salt upward** to the root zone and the surface. ✅ Khullar's own sentence on the Indira Gandhi Canal, at folio 211, is the Indian statement of that outcome: *"Although Indira Gandhi canal in Rajasthan has turned the sandy desert into a granary, it has given birth to serious problems of salinity and alkalinity."*

### 4. ❌ Correction box — the Rann of Kachchh is not another Thar dune field

> ❗ **The disagreement, stated openly, because it is a disagreement between this edition's own sources.**
>
> ✅ **The book position.** The advanced owner records that Khullar *"treats the Rann of Kachchh as an EXTENSION of the Thar desert"*, and lists as a trap the belief that *"the Rann of Kachchh is unrelated to the Thar."*
>
> ✅ **The canonical correction.** The same advanced owner then states the geomorphic position in its own words: older regional descriptions *"link it with the Rann of Kachchh, but the Rann is a distinct saline mudflat/marsh system, not simply another field of Thar dunes."* Its attribute table repeats it: *"Arid-region linkage, but geomorphically a saline mudflat/marsh distinct from sandy Thar."*
>
> ✅ **The independent corroboration.** The repository map bank's Rann of Kutch row reads *"Seasonal salt marsh, seismicity and flamingo habitat"*, and its trap column states flatly: ❗ *"Not a conventional sand desert."* Its confusable-pair line separates the **Thar** — *"Monsoon-margin hot desert"* — from the Great Rann, noting that *"Great Rann is a salt marsh."* The Sir Creek row places the **Kutch marshes** on the India-Pakistan boundary sector and records that *"It is a tidal estuary, not a river boundary throughout."*
>
> ⚠️ **The resolution, which keeps both halves true.** ❗ **Regionally linked, geomorphically distinct.** The Rann belongs to the same **arid region** as the Thar — it is arid, it is adjacent, it receives the Luni, and a regional geographer describing western India will treat them together. ❗ But it is a **different landform system**: a seasonally inundated **saline mudflat and marsh**, close to sea level and subject to **marine as well as terrestrial inundation**, with an **evaporite surface** rather than an **aeolian sand surface**.
>
> ⚠️ **Why the distinction is a budget distinction.** ❗ The Thar's dune fields are built by **aeolian deposition of sand**; the Rann's surface is built by **evaporative concentration of a seasonal water body**. ❗ The Thar's degradation question is about **sand mobility and cover**; the Rann's is about **salinity, inundation regime and tidal influence**. ⚠️ Treating them as one surface makes both questions unanswerable.
>
> ❗ **Examination form, usable verbatim.** *"The Rann of Kachchh is regionally continuous with the Thar's arid tract and receives the Luni, but it is geomorphically a seasonally inundated saline mudflat and marsh rather than a dune field, and the repository map bank records it explicitly as not a conventional sand desert."*

### 5. The world analogues, named and routed

⚠️ Two objective demands touch closed-basin desiccation, and both are owned elsewhere. ❗ They are named here **as demand evidence only** — they show what is asked about, never what any answer was.

- 🔁 **A6 — 2022 Prelims, Question 23**, on a **West African lake drying into desert**, routed by the ledger to **Geography Topic 34, world regional geography**. ⚠️ The mechanism it tests is exactly this lesson's: a large shallow closed-basin lake in a semi-arid belt, whose **area is extremely sensitive to small changes in inflow** because its margins are nearly flat. ❗ **Named as an analogue; not taught. Its official key is unavailable locally and no answer is recorded or inferred.**
- 🔁 **A7 — 2026 Prelims, Question 39**, on a **desert lake's geography and its heritage status**, routed to **Geography Topic 09, lakes**. ⚠️ It marks the boundary between this lesson and the lakes owner: ❗ **this file explains why salt accumulates in a closed basin and does not classify lakes.** ❗ **The locally held 2026 key is explicitly provisional, and no answer letter is recorded, inferred or hinted at.**

⚠️ **The transferable principle, which is what makes the analogues worth naming:** ❗ **a shallow closed-basin lake with flat margins converts a small hydrological change into a very large areal change.** Sambhar's seasonal contraction from about 225 square kilometres is the Indian demonstration of the same geometry.

### 6. 🇮🇳 India example — the salt system's extent, and its limitation

✅ **The national context, from Khullar, folios 208 to 209:** soil **salinity and alkalinity affect about 80 lakh hectares, roughly 2.43 per cent of India's area**. ✅ He distributes the problem across four distinct settings: **canal-irrigated tracts** of Uttar Pradesh, Punjab and Haryana; **arid Rajasthan**; **semi-arid** Maharashtra, Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh, Telangana and Karnataka; and **coastal** Odisha, Gujarat and West Bengal — with **western Uttar Pradesh worst affected**. ✅ The Indian vocabulary of salt-affected land is recorded on the same folios: **reh, kallar, usar, thur, rakar, karl, chopan**.

⚠️ **Four settings, three different mechanisms, and that is the examinable structure.** ❗ The **arid** case is natural accumulation in closed basins — this lesson. ❗ The **canal-irrigated** case is a raised water table mobilising stored salt — Lesson 16. ❗ The **coastal** case is marine intrusion. ⚠️ A single word, *salinity*, covers three distinct processes, which is a smaller version of the definitional problem Lesson 15 confronts head-on.

❗ **The limitations, three of them.**

- ⚠️ **No date, agency or method** accompanies Khullar's 80 lakh hectare figure or Table 7.2's state values. ❗ They are **source-era book figures** and are quoted as such, with the table number attached. ❗ Lesson 14 audits this class of figure properly.
- ⚠️ **No salt-budget measurement exists for any Indian basin** in the sources available here — no inflow solute load, no accumulation rate, no estimate of total stored salt for Sambhar or for the Rann. ❗ **The budget argument above is a qualitative identity, not a quantified balance**, and this file does not pretend otherwise.
- ⚠️ **No area is given for the inland-drainage tract** itself, and ✅ Khullar names six lakes while writing *"are some of the important lakes"* — ❗ so the list is **explicitly non-exhaustive** and should be quoted with that hedge.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Thar dune field | Inland saline lake basin | Rann of Kachchh |
|---|---|---|---|
| Surface material | ✅ Aeolian **sand**; shifting dunes called **dhrian** in the west | Lake-bed sediment with an **evaporite crust** on drying | ✅ **Saline mudflat and marsh** |
| Dominant process | **Aeolian** erosion, transport and deposition | **Evaporative concentration** in a closed basin | Seasonal **inundation and evaporation**, with marine influence |
| Elevation | ✅ Marusthali **200-250 m** | ✅ Sambhar at about **360 m**, *"in the Aravali terrain"* | Near sea level |
| Water | Essentially absent at the surface | ✅ Ephemeral; Sambhar *"about 225 sq km"* in the rains, shrinking in the dry season | Seasonally inundated |
| Relation to the Luni | The Luni crosses the tract | ❗ **Separate system**, north of the Luni basin | ✅ The Luni's **terminus** |
| Degradation question | Sand mobility, cover loss, deflation | Water abstraction, inflow change, salt-extraction pressure | Salinity, inundation regime, tidal influence |
| Map-bank trap line | ✅ *"Monsoon-margin hot desert"* | ✅ Sambhar — *"Not a coastal lagoon"* | ❗ ✅ *"Not a conventional sand desert"* |

**Strongest objection.** This is drainage and lakes, owned by other topics. An arid-landforms file should stop at the dune.

**Reply.** The Basic owner's own ownership clause names **playas** in the owned core and its terminology control names **playa against the wider desert** as a distinction this topic owns. ❗ More decisively, **salinity is a degradation pathway**, and the owned 2026 demand's prescribed route requires *land-productivity effects* and *controls*. ⚠️ Salinity is one of the pathways that makes the owned answer's **limiting argument** possible: aeolian action is **one** route to lost land capability, and the salt route runs through this lesson. ❗ What is **not** taken is clear: **no lake classification, no lake-death sequence, no groundwater mechanics, no canal engineering**, and no treatment of the Rann beyond the correction and its placement.

⚠️ **Residual.** Whether the Rann's salt is **predominantly marine or predominantly terrestrial in origin** is not resolved by any source available to this edition, and the question is a real one given that the Rann is both close to sea level and the terminus of a terrestrial drainage system. ❗ No apportionment is stated anywhere in this file, and the Rann is described by **what its surface is** rather than by where its salt came from.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** *"Geographical features and their location-changes"*, General Studies Paper I; the physical-geography clause for the closed-basin mechanism. 🔁 Lake classification routed to Topic 09; world regional identification routed to Topic 34.
- **Verified linkage:** ✅ **2026 General Studies Paper I, Question 6** — this lesson supplies a **non-aeolian degradation pathway**, which the owned answer's limiting argument requires. 🔁 **A6 (2022 Prelims Q23)** and 🔁 **A7 (2026 Prelims Q39)** are named as adjacent demands owned elsewhere; ❗ **no key is recorded or inferred for either.**
- **Traps:** saying the Luni reaches the Arabian Sea; calling the Rann a sand desert or an extension of the dune field; placing the inland-drainage lakes in the arid core rather than on the eastern, higher margin; treating a shrinking playa as evidence of desiccation when seasonal contraction is its normal behaviour; treating all Indian salinity as irrigation-induced.
- **Answer use:** the budget sentence travels anywhere a closed basin appears. *"In an endorheic basin water leaves only as vapour and vapour carries no solutes, so salt can only accumulate; the lake's area oscillates seasonally while its salt mass does not fall, which is why Rajasthan's inland lakes are a commercial salt source and why irrigation there risks mobilising a store that is already present."*

Revision notes:

- ❗ 🔁 **The L8 identity, applied:** inputs carry solutes · outflow is nil · evaporation removes water only · therefore **salt accumulates**.
- ✅ **The Luni:** *"a seasonal stream which flows towards the south-west to the Rann of Kachchh."* ✅ Map bank trap: *"Does not normally reach the Arabian Sea as a perennial river."*
- ✅ **The closed tract:** *"North of the Luni basin, there is a large area of inland drainage on the eastern edge of the Thar Desert having several saline lakes… a great source of common salt and many other salts."*
- ✅ **The six named lakes:** **Sambhar · Didwana · Degana · Kuchaman · Sargol · Khatu.** ❗ Khullar's own hedge: *"are some of the important lakes"* — the list is **not exhaustive**.
- ✅ **Sambhar:** about **65 km west of Jaipur**; altitude about **360 m**, *"in the Aravali terrain"*; about **30 km long**, average width **3-8 km**; *"about 225 sq km during the rainy season but shrinks considerably in dry season."* ✅ Map-bank trap: *"Not a coastal lagoon."*
- ❗ **Sambhar is a playa in all but name.** ⚠️ Area oscillates; **salt mass does not fall**.
- ❌ **Rann correction, mandatory.** ✅ Advanced owner: the Rann is *"a distinct saline mudflat/marsh system, not simply another field of Thar dunes"*, while ✅ Khullar *"treats the Rann of Kachchh as an EXTENSION of the Thar desert."* ✅ Map bank: *"Not a conventional sand desert"*; *"Great Rann is a salt marsh."* ❗ **Resolution: regionally linked, geomorphically distinct.**
- ✅ **Sir Creek** (map bank): Kutch marshes, India-Pakistan boundary sector; *"It is a tidal estuary, not a river boundary throughout."*
- ✅ **National salinity (Khullar, folios 208-209):** salinity and alkalinity affect about **80 lakh ha ≈ 2.43 % of India**; four settings — **canal-irrigated** UP, Punjab, Haryana · **arid** Rajasthan · **semi-arid** Maharashtra, Gujarat, AP, Telangana, Karnataka · **coastal** Odisha, Gujarat, West Bengal; **western Uttar Pradesh worst**.
- ✅ **Indian salt-land vocabulary:** **reh, kallar, usar, thur, rakar, karl, chopan**.
- ✅ **Table 7.2 anchor:** Rajasthan **7.28 lakh ha**, against UP **12.95**, Punjab **12.25**, Gujarat **12.14**. ⚠️ Rajasthan's share has a **natural accumulation component**.
- ✅ **Canal trade-off (Khullar, folio 211):** *"Although Indira Gandhi canal in Rajasthan has turned the sandy desert into a granary, it has given birth to serious problems of salinity and alkalinity."* 🔁 Developed at L16.
- 🔁 **Routed, not taught:** **A6 (2022 Prelims Q23)**, West African lake drying into desert — Topic 34; **A7 (2026 Prelims Q39)**, desert lake and heritage status — Topic 09. ❗ **No key recorded or inferred.**
- ❗ **Limitations:** no date or agency on the salinity figures; **no salt-budget measurement** for any Indian basin; **no area** for the inland-drainage tract.

### Concept check

**Question:** A proposal argues that the Rann of Kachchh should be managed under the same programme as the Thar's dune fields, "since the Rann is simply the southern extension of the desert." Evaluate the premise and state what each system's degradation question actually is.

**Model answer:** The premise is **half right and operationally misleading**. It is right regionally: the Rann is arid, adjacent to the Thar, receives the seasonal Luni as its terminus, and older regional descriptions — including the one this edition's own book source uses — treat it as an extension of the desert. It is wrong geomorphically, and the advanced canonical owner states the correction explicitly: the Rann is a **distinct saline mudflat and marsh system, not simply another field of Thar dunes**, a position the repository map bank corroborates independently by recording it as **not a conventional sand desert** and as a seasonal salt marsh. The two systems are built by different processes. The Thar's dune fields are products of **aeolian deposition of sand**, with surfaces that are mobile where unvegetated and fixed where vegetated; their degradation question is about **sand mobility, cover loss and deflation**, and the appropriate measures are barriers, plantation, grazing control and pasture development. The Rann's surface is a product of **seasonal inundation followed by evaporative concentration**, lies close to sea level, and is subject to **marine as well as terrestrial influence**; its degradation question is about **salinity, the inundation regime and tidal effects**, for which dune stabilisation is simply not an instrument. Treating them under one programme would apply sand measures to a mudflat and salt measures to a dune field, and would fail on both.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not infer geomorphic identity from regional adjacency or from a shared climatic class. Two arid surfaces a hundred kilometres apart can be built by entirely different processes, and the test is what the surface is made of and how it was emplaced, not whether the same regional chapter describes both.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Salt in India's arid zone is accumulated, not imported." Examine, with reference to inland drainage. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The statement holds for the natural component but needs qualification for the induced. On the eastern edge of the Thar, north of the Luni basin, lies a large area of inland drainage with no outlet to the sea. Every input — rainfall, runoff from the weathering Aravalli, groundwater seepage — carries dissolved solids, while the only significant output is evaporation, and vapour carries no solutes. Salt mass can therefore only rise. The result is the saline-lake cluster Khullar names, including Sambhar, which occupies about two hundred and twenty-five square kilometres in the rains and shrinks considerably in the dry season while remaining a commercial salt source. The qualification is that irrigation does import water, not salt: by raising the water table it mobilises stored salt upward, which is why Khullar records the Indira Gandhi Canal turning sandy desert into a granary while giving birth to salinity and alkalinity.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 2 for the closed-basin condition stated as the premise; 3 for the budget argument — solute-bearing inputs, solute-free evaporation, no outflow — derived rather than asserted; 2 for the named Indian system with at least one sourced measurement; 2 for the irrigation qualification distinguishing relocation from import; 1 for the seasonal-area-against-salt-mass distinction. Deduct 2 for treating Indian salinity as a single process.

Canonical basis: Basic owner §8.1 — the five independent, largely physical causes of aridity; §8.4 — *"the reversibility point: dryland degradation is often recoverable through vegetation restoration, shelterbelts, controlled grazing and water harvesting — which is why 'advancing desert' imagery is misleading"*, together with its factual caution against quoting a rate of desert advance from memory; the **causal-control clause** — *"wind is not the sole geomorphic agent and people are not inherently a degradation driver."* The subject master framework's anti-determinism line, under which **this topic is the first named**: *"Physical constraint vs human adaptation: climate/relief/resource constraints shape but do not fully determine human settlement and development choices."*
Book basis: D.R. Khullar, local scan PDF p.123, folio 212, for the desertification definition, the *"partly natural but largely man made"* claim, the half-kilometre-a-year advance statement and the causes list. Majid Husain, *Geography of India*, printed p.2.28 (local scan PDF p.52), for the determinism specimen *"Had there been no Himalayas, the whole of northern India would have been a desert"* — ❗ used **only** as a specimen of a form of reasoning, and this volume is **not** used as a Thar source anywhere in this file.

Boundary: 🔁 Topic 18 owns the desert climatic regime and its history as climate; 🔁 the environment-and-ecology owner owns desertification as a convention and a policy instrument; 🔁 the archaeology and ancient-history owners own the human occupation record. ❗ **This lesson owns only the question of how a physical claim about origin would be tested, and what the available evidence does and does not support.**
Trap: determinism in both directions — "the desert is natural and nothing can be done" and "the desert is man made and can be pushed back". Both are unsupported, and the sources contain specimens of each.
