The strip is the lesson. Before asking whether the Thar is man-made, ask what evidence would answer the question — and then notice that the sources making the loudest claims do not cite any of it.

### 1. The two questions that are constantly conflated

⚠️ "Is the Thar natural or man-made?" is really **two separate questions**, and almost every confused answer comes from merging them.

- ❗ **Question A — origin of aridity.** *Why is this region dry?* ⚠️ 🔁 **Lesson 10 answered this**, and the answer is **climatic**: subtropical position supplying a high evaporative demand, continentality, monsoon geometry delivering moisture with weak lifting, and an Aravalli alignment offering little forced ascent. ❗ **None of these is a human variable.** Human action does not create a monsoon margin.
- ❗ **Question B — condition of the surface.** *Given that the region is dry, how much vegetation, how much mobile sand, how much productive capacity does it have, and how have those changed?* ⚠️ This question is **substantially human**, because cover, grazing pressure, cultivation extent, fuelwood offtake and water management all act directly on the surface.

❗ **The resolution that keeps both true, and it is the examinable formulation:** ⚠️ **aridity's existence is a climatic outcome; the severity, surface condition and productivity of the arid land are substantially human-modified.** ❗ A candidate who writes that sentence has answered the question correctly and has not conceded either pole.

### 2. ❓ Open-question box — "partly natural but largely man made"

> ✅ **The claim, quoted exactly.** D.R. Khullar, local scan PDF p.123, folio 212: *"The Rajasthan desert is partly natural but largely man made and the fear is often expressed, quite rightly too, that the desert is spreading and that conditions within the desert are deteriorating."*
>
> ✅ **The causes the same page attributes:** *"The process of desertification is attributed to various causes of which more important are uncontrolled grazing, reckless felling of trees and growing population. Climate change have also contributed to the spread of deserts."*
>
> ⚠️ **What supports the claim.** ❗ The listed causes are **real mechanisms with real effects**, and this file has already built every one of them: 🔁 **Lesson 3** showed that removing cover lowers two rungs of the supply ladder; 🔁 **Lesson 4** showed that bared surfaces entrain and that the saltation cascade escalates; 🔁 **Lesson 9** showed that breaking a desert pavement restarts deflation at full rate. ❗ **That human action degrades a dryland surface is not in dispute anywhere in this file.**
>
> ⚠️ **What does not support the claim.** ❗ The word **"largely"** is a **quantitative apportionment**, and Khullar supplies **no apportionment evidence** — no chronology, no dated dune ages, no pollen record, no occupation-density series, no counterfactual. ❗ And it is **in tension with the same book's own facing material**: on the very page that calls the desert largely man-made, Khullar attributes north-western aridity to *"long distances from the sea"* — a physical cause — and records that *"about 12.13 per cent of India's total area is classed as arid"*, a climatic classification.
>
> ⚠️ **What the canonical owner holds.** The Basic owner gives **five independent, largely physical causes of aridity**, and its causal-control clause states that **"people are not inherently a degradation driver"** and that the effects of cover removal, grazing intensity, tillage, groundwater stress, mining, infrastructure, irrigation, drainage and rainfall variability *"interact differently by site and timescale."*
>
> ⚠️ **The framework's own position.** The subject master framework names this topic **first** under its anti-determinism thesis: *"climate/relief/resource constraints shape but do not fully determine human settlement and development choices."* ❗ The reverse determinism — that human choices fully determine the physical condition — is the same error inverted.
>
> ❗ **Disposition: neither pole is asserted in this edition.** ⚠️ The claim is reported **with its source and page**, the physical-cause framing is set beside it, and the resolution offered is the two-question separation in section 1. ❗ **Do not write that the Thar is man-made. Do not write that human action is irrelevant to its condition.** ⚠️ Write the separation, and the answer is both defensible and distinctive.

### 3. ❌ Correction box — the advancing desert, and the four statements that must all be reported

> ❗ **This is the single most important correction in the topic**, because four sources that this edition treats as authoritative say materially different things, and three of them are recent and official.
>
> ✅ **Statement one — the textbook rate.** Khullar, folio 212: *"It has been estimated that the Thar Desert is advancing at an alarming rate of about 0.5 km per year."* ⚠️ The same paragraph supplies a mechanism for the image: *"The sand carried by wind is deposited on the adjoining fertile lands whose fertility dwindles and sometimes the fertile land merges with the advancing desert."*
>
> ⚠️ **Statement two — the canonical correction.** Basic owner §8.4: *"The reversibility point: dryland degradation is often recoverable through vegetation restoration, shelterbelts, controlled grazing and water harvesting — which is why 'advancing desert' imagery is misleading."* The same section adds a factual caution: ❗ do **not** quote *"a rate of desert advance"* from memory.
>
> 📰 ✅ **Statement three — the programme rationale.** The Press Information Bureau release of **25 March 2023** launching the Aravalli Green Wall Project lists among its objectives: *"To prevent eastward expansion of Thar Desert and to reduce land degradation by creating green barriers that will prevent soil erosion, desertification and dust storms."*
>
> 📰 ✅ **Statement four — the judicial framing.** The Press Information Bureau Research Unit backgrounder of **21 December 2025** records that the Supreme Court *"emphasized the ecological importance of the Aravalli range as a barrier against desertification, a groundwater recharge zone, and a biodiversity habitat,"* and that the Aravallis are recognised *"across 37 districts by state governments, with their ecological role noted as a natural barrier against northern desertification."*
>
> ⚠️ **How to hold all four without contradiction, and this is the analytic move the topic turns on.**
>
> ❗ **The textbook rate is an assertion without apparatus.** It carries **no issuing agency, no measurement method, no baseline date and no definition of what is advancing** — the sand surface, the arid climatic class, or the degraded-land condition class? ⚠️ Those are three different things moving in three different ways, and a single figure in kilometres per year does not say which. ❗ **This edition does not repeat 0.5 kilometres a year as a fact.** It is reported as a sourced claim and audited.
>
> ❗ **The canonical correction is about what is being measured, not about whether degradation is real.** ⚠️ Degradation is **a loss of land capability in place**, detected as a **change in condition class over an area**, not as a front crossing a map. ❗ India's own national measurement is of exactly this kind — 🔁 Lesson 10 established and Lesson 15 develops that the atlas reports **degraded and desertified area by class, state and process for a mapped period**, not a desert outline advancing eastward.
>
> ❗ **The programme and judicial statements are rationales, not measurements.** ⚠️ They are **genuine, dated, first-party statements of why a public action was taken** and of how a court characterised an ecological asset. ❗ They are evidence of **what the state and the court hold**; they are not evidence of a measured rate of advance, and neither document supplies one. ❗ **Report them; do not convert them into a geomorphic finding, and do not ridicule them.**
>
> ⚠️ **The reconciliation, usable verbatim.** *"Both the Aravalli Green Wall's stated objective of preventing the eastward expansion of the Thar and the Supreme Court's description of the range as a barrier against desertification are dated first-party rationales for public action. The canonical geomorphic position is that advancing-desert imagery is misleading, because what is measured is a loss of land capability in place, reported in condition classes. The two are compatible if the programme is read as treating degradation along a vulnerable margin rather than as halting a moving frontier — and that reading also explains why the project works on a five-kilometre buffer of scrubland, wasteland and degraded forest rather than on a line."*
>
> ⚠️ **Why the tension is worth more than either side alone.** ❗ It is a live, dated, examinable case of the gap between **policy language and measurement language** — and that gap is precisely what an examiner asking candidates to *discuss* a process wants to see handled.

### 4. ❌ Correction box — the Aravalli as a transition, not a wall

> ⚠️ ✅ **The framing in the sources.** Khullar's folio 92 gives the Western plain *"a well defined boundary in the east marked by the Aravalli range"*. ✅ The repository map bank's Aravalli row carries the phrase **"desert barrier"**. 📰 ✅ CA-4 records the judicial adoption of the same framing — *"a barrier against desertification"* and *"a natural barrier against northern desertification."*
>
> ❗ **The testable version, which is what an answer should give.** ⚠️ The Aravalli is a **topographic and ecological transition** that **coincides with** a climatic boundary and marks **the eastern limit of continuous dune cover**. ✅ Khullar's own physiography supports exactly this and no more: the Bagar runs *"from the edge of the Aravali in the east to the 25 cm isohyet in the west"*, and 🔁 Lesson 11's dune gradient falls from a mean of about **30 metres at Barmer** to about **8 metres near Jaipur and Sikar**.
>
> ❗ **What the barrier framing cannot mean.** ⚠️ 🔁 Lesson 10 established that the range is aligned **south-west to north-east, along the monsoon flow rather than across it**, so it is **not an orographic barrier in the rainfall sense**. ❗ And a ridge cannot stop a process that is **a loss of capability in place** rather than a moving object. ⚠️ What a vegetated, intact range **can** do is real and worth stating: it **reduces near-surface wind speed locally**, it **traps saltating and creeping sand**, it **supplies the seasonal streams that water the Bagar**, and it **recharges groundwater** — the function CA-4 records the Court as emphasising.
>
> ❗ **Examination form.** *"The Aravalli is best described as the eastern limit of continuous dune cover and a topographic and ecological transition coinciding with a rainfall gradient, rather than as a wall that stops a moving desert — its measurable functions are local wind reduction, sand trapping, seasonal stream supply and groundwater recharge."*

### 5. ⚠️ The determinism specimen, used once and attributed

⚠️ The sources contain a clean specimen of the reasoning this topic is named first to resist. ✅ Majid Husain, *Geography of India*, printed p.2.28 (local scan PDF p.52), under the heading *"Climatic Influence"*: *"The altitude of the Himalayas, their sprawl and extension intercept the summer monsoon coming from the Bay of Bengal and the Arabian Sea. They also prevent the cold Siberian air masses from entering into India. Had there been no Himalayas, the whole of northern India would have been a desert."*

❗ **This is reported as a specimen, not asserted.** ⚠️ The form of the sentence is a **counterfactual with a single physical cause and a total outcome** — remove one landform, and an entire subcontinental region becomes a desert. ❗ Three things are wrong with it as a method, and naming them is the transferable skill:

1. ⚠️ It treats one control as **sufficient**, when 🔁 Lesson 1 established that aridity is produced by **several interacting routes**.
2. ⚠️ It asserts a **counterfactual that cannot be tested**, since there is no comparable monsoon system without a comparable barrier.
3. ⚠️ It slides from **influence** to **determination** — the Himalaya's effect on Indian climate is enormous and uncontested, but *"would have been a desert"* is a quantified claim about a world that does not exist.

❗ **This volume is used for nothing else in this file.** ⚠️ Its printed pages covering the Rajasthan Plain are **absent from the local scan**, so it contributes no Thar physiography at all — a gap disclosed in the source ledger.

### 6. 🇮🇳 India example — what the state actually did, and its limitation

📰 ✅ **CA-3, in its own terms.** The project *"covers states of Haryana, Rajasthan, Gujarat and Delhi — where the Aravalli hills landscape span over 6 million hectares of land"*, and greens a **5 kilometre buffer area around the Aravalli Hill Range**. ✅ The method is *"planting native species of trees and shrubs on scrubland, wasteland and degraded forest land, along with rejuvenating and restoring surface water bodies such as ponds, lakes and streams"*, together with *"agroforestry and pasture development to enhance the livelihoods of local communities."* ✅ On the same day the Ministry unveiled the **National Action Plan to Combat Desertification and Land Degradation Through Forestry Interventions** and a **FAQ on agroforestry published by the Indian Council of Forestry Research and Education**.

⚠️ **Read the method against the rationale and the tension partly dissolves.** ❗ The stated objective speaks of **preventing eastward expansion**; the stated method works on **scrubland, wasteland and degraded forest land across a six-million-hectare landscape**, restores **water bodies**, and develops **agroforestry and pasture**. ❗ **That is land-condition treatment over an area, which is exactly what the canonical position says degradation requires** — not a linear barrier against a frontier.

❗ **The limitations, four of them, all material.**

- ❗ **The widely circulated corridor-length figure is excluded.** ⚠️ A "1,400 kilometre long" description of the Aravalli Green Wall circulates widely; ❗ **it does not appear in the first-party release**, which gives only the **5 kilometre buffer**, the **four states** and the **approximately 6 million hectare landscape**. ❗ **No corridor length is stated anywhere in this file**, and if one is needed the honest answer is that the first-party release does not give one.
- ⚠️ **CA-4's legal definition is about mining regulation, not about desert advance.** ✅ It records that **only Rajasthan had a formal mining-regulation definition**, based on its **2002 Committee Report** applying the **Richard Murphy landform classification** — *"all landforms rising 100 m above local relief are hills"* — in force since **9 January 2006**; that **all States agreed to adopt the uniform "100 metres above local relief" criterion**; that *"Aravali range has been explained as all the landforms which exists within 500 metres of two adjoining Hills of height 100 metre or more"*; and that a buffer of **no mining within 1.0 kilometre** applies. ⚠️ ❗ That is a **definitional and regulatory instrument**, and it is dated: the Committee was constituted per an order of **9 May 2024**, with further directions of **12 August 2025**, comprising forest secretaries of **Delhi, Haryana, Rajasthan and Gujarat** with the **Forest Survey of India, the Central Empowered Committee and the Geological Survey of India**. ❗ **Anything after 21 December 2025 is outside this edition's evidence and is not asserted.**
- ⚠️ **No outcome is claimed.** ❗ Neither release reports **measured restoration**, measured dune stabilisation, or measured change in degradation class attributable to the project. ❗ **A launch is not an outcome**, and this file does not treat it as one.
- ⚠️ **No Thar-specific state-wise degradation figure was obtained first-party.** ❗ The advanced owner's statements that **Rajasthan has the largest desertified area** and that **wind erosion drives more than 40 per cent of its desertification** rest on that owner alone; ❗ the 94.8 megabyte atlas publication itself was **not downloaded**, and **no percentage is attached to Rajasthan's total anywhere in this file**. ⚠️ The **ranking** and the **aeolian-share statement** are used with the owner named and the period given, and no further.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| Proposition | Status in this edition | Why |
|---|---|---|
| The Thar's **aridity** is climatically caused | ⚠️ **Held** | Five independent physical routes; no human variable creates a monsoon margin (🔁 L1, L10) |
| The Thar's **surface condition** is substantially human-modified | ⚠️ **Held** | Mechanisms built at L3, L4, L9; causes named by Khullar at folio 212 |
| The Thar is **"largely man made"** | ❓ **Reported, not asserted** | A quantitative apportionment with no apportionment evidence in any source here |
| The Thar **advances at about 0.5 km per year** | ❌ **Reported, not repeated as fact** | No agency, no method, no baseline, no definition of what advances |
| Degradation is **a loss of capability in place** | ⚠️ **Held** | Canonical position; corroborated by India's own class-based national measurement |
| The Aravalli is a **barrier against desertification** | ⚠️ **Reported as a stated rationale**; the testable version is given | A ridge cannot halt a process of in-place capability loss; its real functions are local and are stated |
| Dryland degradation is **often reversible** | ⚠️ **Held, with a caveat** | Canonical position; ❗ but **no recovery timescale** exists in any source here (🔁 L9) |

**Strongest objection.** Refusing to take a position is evasion. The examiner wants a judgement, and "neither pole is asserted" reads as a candidate who has not decided.

**Reply.** A judgement **is** being made, and it is a sharper one than either pole. ❗ The judgement is that **the question as usually posed is malformed**, because it merges an origin question whose answer is climatic with a condition question whose answer is substantially human. ⚠️ Separating them is not fence-sitting; it is the **only** way to say something true about both. ❗ And the separation has a practical payoff that an examiner can see: it predicts that **restoration is possible for condition and impossible for aridity**, which is exactly why the Basic owner holds degradation to be often reversible while nobody proposes to make western Rajasthan humid. ⚠️ A candidate who writes *"the aridity cannot be reversed and the condition can"* has converted a philosophical dispute into an operational statement.

⚠️ **Residual, and it is a large one.** ❗ **No chronology of the Thar's development is available to this edition at any time depth.** ⚠️ There are no dated dune ages, no palaeo-lake levels, no pollen sequence, no archaeological occupation series and no repeat-survey record in any source consulted. ❗ **The origin question is therefore genuinely open on this evidence base**, and the strip at the head of this lesson exists to say so precisely rather than vaguely. ⚠️ The only quantitative change evidence that **is** available is the atlas's **two-epoch comparison**, and 🔁 Lesson 15 shows exactly how little that can be made to carry.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** *"Geographical features and their location-changes… and the effects of such changes"*, General Studies Paper I; the subject master framework's anti-determinism line, under which this topic is first named. 🔁 Policy and convention routed to the environment-and-ecology owner; climatic regime routed to Topic 18.
- **Verified linkage:** ✅ **2026 General Studies Paper I, Question 6.** This lesson supplies the **human amplification** element with its canonical caveat — people are not inherently a degradation driver — and it supplies the qualification that separates a six-mark answer from a nine-mark one. 🔁 It also prepares the bounded cross-owner use of the **2020 General Studies Paper I demand on desertification as a process without climatic boundaries**, which is owned by the environment-and-ecology file and is handled at Lesson 15.
- **Traps:** repeating the half-kilometre-a-year rate as fact; writing that the Thar is man-made; writing that human action is irrelevant; treating a programme objective or a judicial observation as a geomorphic measurement; using the circulating corridor-length figure for the Green Wall; treating the Aravalli as an orographic or frontal barrier; asserting any Thar chronology.
- **Answer use:** two sentences, and they are among the most valuable in the file. *"Aridity's existence in western Rajasthan is a climatic outcome of several interacting controls; its severity, surface condition and productivity are substantially human-modified."* And: *"Programme and judicial statements describing the Aravalli as preventing the eastward expansion of the Thar are dated first-party rationales for action, while the geomorphic position is that degradation is a loss of land capability in place, measured in condition classes rather than as a moving frontier."*

Revision notes:

- ❗ **Two questions, never merge them:** **(A)** why is it dry — **climatic**; **(B)** what condition is the surface in — **substantially human**.
- ❗ **The resolution, verbatim:** *aridity's existence is a climatic outcome; its severity, surface condition and productivity are substantially human-modified.*
- ❓ **[N2] Open question.** ✅ Khullar, folio 212: *"The Rajasthan desert is partly natural but largely man made and the fear is often expressed, quite rightly too, that the desert is spreading."* ❗ *"Largely"* is an apportionment with **no apportionment evidence**. ⚠️ Same page attributes NW aridity to **distance from the sea** and classes **12.13 %** of India as arid.
- ✅ **Khullar's causes:** *"uncontrolled grazing, reckless felling of trees and growing population. Climate change have also contributed."*
- ⚠️ **Causal control (Basic owner):** *"wind is not the sole geomorphic agent and people are not inherently a degradation driver"*; drivers *"interact differently by site and timescale."*
- ❌ **[N1] The advancing-desert correction — four statements, all reported:** ✅ Khullar — *"advancing at an alarming rate of about 0.5 km per year"*; ⚠️ Basic owner — *"'advancing desert' imagery is misleading"*, degradation *"often recoverable"*; 📰 CA-3 (25 Mar 2023) — *"To prevent eastward expansion of Thar Desert"*; 📰 CA-4 (21 Dec 2025) — the Court on the Aravalli *"as a barrier against desertification."*
- ❗ **The rate carries no agency, no method, no baseline and no definition of what advances.** ❗ **Not repeated as fact anywhere in this file.**
- ❗ **Policy rationale ≠ geomorphic measurement.** Report both; **do not ridicule the programme**; do not convert a rationale into a finding.
- ❗ **[N13] The Aravalli, testable version:** a **topographic and ecological transition** coinciding with a rainfall gradient and marking the **eastern limit of continuous dune cover**. ⚠️ Real functions: **local wind reduction · sand trapping · seasonal stream supply · groundwater recharge**. ❗ Not an orographic barrier (SW-NE, along the flow) and not a wall against a frontier.
- ⚠️ **Determinism specimen, attributed, used once:** ✅ Majid, *Geography of India*, printed 2.28 — *"Had there been no Himalayas, the whole of northern India would have been a desert."* ❗ Single sufficient cause · untestable counterfactual · influence slid into determination.
- 📰 ✅ **CA-3 detail:** **Haryana, Rajasthan, Gujarat, Delhi**; **5 km buffer**; landscape *"span over 6 million hectares"*; **native species on scrubland, wasteland and degraded forest land**; **water-body rejuvenation**; **agroforestry and pasture development**; **National Action Plan to Combat Desertification and Land Degradation Through Forestry Interventions** unveiled the same day with an **ICFRE agroforestry FAQ**.
- ❗ **[N11] The circulating "1,400 km" corridor figure is EXCLUDED** — absent from the first-party release. ❗ No corridor length is stated anywhere in this file.
- 📰 ✅ **CA-4 detail:** Court order of **November-December 2025**; Committee constituted per order of **9 May 2024**, further directions **12 Aug 2025**; forest secretaries of **Delhi, Haryana, Rajasthan, Gujarat** plus **FSI, Central Empowered Committee, GSI**; Aravallis recognised across **37 districts**; **Richard Murphy** landform classification, *"all landforms rising 100 m above local relief are hills"*, in force in Rajasthan since **09.01.2006**; range defined as landforms *"within 500 metres of two adjoining Hills of height 100 metre or more"*; **no mining within 1.0 km**. ❗ Dated **21 December 2025**; nothing later is asserted.
- ❗ **No outcome is claimed** for either programme. ❗ **No Thar chronology** of any kind exists in these sources — no dune ages, no pollen, no lake levels, no occupation series.
- ❗ **[U5]** *Rajasthan has the largest desertified area* and *wind erosion drives 40 %+ of its desertification* rest on the **advanced owner alone**; the atlas publication was **not downloaded**; ❗ **no percentage is attached to Rajasthan's total**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A newspaper reports: "Scientists say the Thar is marching east at half a kilometre a year, which is why the government has built a green wall and why the Supreme Court calls the Aravalli a barrier against desertification." Identify what in that sentence is sourced, what is unsourced, and what a geographer should write instead.

**Model answer:** Three claims are bundled and they have different evidential standing. The **green wall and the court's description are genuine and datable**. The Aravalli Green Wall Project was launched by the Ministry on 25 March 2023, greening a five-kilometre buffer around the range across four states within a landscape spanning about six million hectares, and its stated objectives do include preventing the eastward expansion of the Thar. A government backgrounder of 21 December 2025 records the Supreme Court emphasising the range's ecological importance as a barrier against desertification, a groundwater recharge zone and a biodiversity habitat. Both are **first-party statements of rationale**, and both should be reported as such. The **half-kilometre-a-year rate is the weak link**. It appears in Khullar as an estimate, with no issuing agency, no method, no baseline date and no statement of what is advancing — a sand surface, an arid climatic class, or a degraded-land condition class, which are three different things. Attributing it to "scientists" adds authority the source does not carry. What a geographer should write instead is that degradation is a **loss of land capability in place**, detected as a change in condition class over an area, which is precisely how India's own national atlas reports it — by class, state and process for a mapped period — and that the canonical owner's geomorphic view is that advancing-desert imagery is misleading. The programme is then intelligible on its own stated method: it treats scrubland, wasteland and degraded forest across a landscape, restores water bodies and develops agroforestry and pasture, which is area-based condition treatment rather than a line held against a frontier.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat a government objective or a judicial observation as a scientific measurement, and do not treat the absence of a measurement as proof that the programme is misconceived. The two registers answer different questions, and a candidate's job is to report both accurately and say which is which.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "The Thar's aridity is natural; its degradation is not." Critically examine. *(Critically examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The statement is broadly defensible with one correction to each half. The aridity is climatic: subtropical position supplies high evaporative demand, continentality contributes, the summer monsoon reaches the north-west with weak lifting so moisture is present while rain is not, and the Aravalli lies along the flow, not across it. No human variable creates a monsoon margin. The degradation half needs qualifying, not endorsing. Khullar attributes it to uncontrolled grazing, reckless felling and growing population, and the mechanisms are real: removing cover and breaking a pavement restart deflation. But the canonical owner holds that people are not inherently a degradation driver and that rainfall variability interacts with land use differently by site and timescale; at a fluctuating monsoon margin, surface condition varies with the year. His claim that the desert is largely man-made is an apportionment unsupported by any chronology. The defensible formulation separates existence from condition.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 4 for the two-question separation stated explicitly — climatic existence against substantially human condition — and used as the organising structure; 2 for at least two physical aridity controls named correctly; 3 for the human mechanisms given with their canonical caveat and the variability confound at a monsoon margin; 1 for identifying the apportionment claim as unsupported. Deduct 3 for repeating a rate of desert advance as fact and 2 for asserting either determinist pole.

## Lesson 14 — Wind erosion and land degradation in India: the evidence and its contradictions

Progress: L14 / 18 — Wind erosion and land degradation in India: the evidence and its contradictions  |  Stage: Applied  |  Band: Heaviest

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Canonical basis: Basic owner §8.4's factual caution — *"do not quote a percentage of India's or the world's land as degraded, a rate of desert advance, or a dune-migration rate from memory"*; the **date and data control** in the ownership clause — *"the SAC atlas reports 2018-19 mapped condition, not a 2026 measurement"*; the causal-control clause that **wind is not the sole geomorphic agent**. Advanced owner §1's atlas anchor and its degradation-driver row.
Book basis: D.R. Khullar, local scan PDF p.119, folios 204 to 205, for the eight problems of Indian soils and **Fig. 7.2 "India: Soil Erosion"** with its legend and its dashed limit-of-wind-erosion line; local scan PDF p.120, folios 206 to 207, for the wind-erosion mechanism in Indian terms, the human factors, **Table 7.1 "Area Under Ravines in India"** and the **extent-of-erosion block transcribed from a 300 dots-per-inch crop**; local scan PDF p.121, folios 208 to 209, for ravines, salinity extent and **Fig. 7.3**; local scan PDF p.122, folios 210 to 211, for **Table 7.2 "Salinity Affected Areas in India"**, **Table 7.3 "Area under Arid and Semi-arid conditions in India"** transcribed from 400 dots-per-inch crops, and **Fig. 7.4**.
CA: ✅ **CA-2** — Press Information Bureau release 1946401, **07 August 2023**: land degradation and desertification *"estimated to be 97.84 million hectares in 2018-19"*, with an **online portal developed with the Space Applications Centre** *"for visualization of degraded area of land with the processes causing degradation."* ✅ **CA-5** — the Space Applications Centre VEDAS Desertification Status Mapping Atlas index, fetched this pass and **undated on its face**, which lists the atlas as a **series**: a Land Degradation Vulnerability Atlas; the *Desertification and Land Degradation Atlas of India 2021*; *Desertification Land Degradation Atlas 2018* Volumes 1 and 2; a Desertification Status Mapping Technical Guideline; and a *Desertification and Land Degradation Atlas of India 2016*, with a separate dashboard listed under applications.
Boundary: 🔁 **Topic 05** owns fluvial process, so ravines and sheet erosion are used here only as **co-equal degradation pathways** for comparison, not developed as landform genesis. 🔁 The **agriculture and soils owners** own conservation agronomy and shifting cultivation. 🔁 The **environment-and-ecology owner** owns the convention and the policy instrument. ❗ **The atlas publication itself was not downloaded**, so **no figure is attributed to it directly**; every national figure here is attributed to the Ministry release that states it.
Trap: quoting an Indian degradation hectare figure as if it were a measurement. This lesson exists to show what such a figure must carry before it is evidence.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### The contradiction ledger — this edition's one pure table

| # | Figure, exactly as the source states it | Source and location | Stated scope | Method, agency, date | What it is incompatible with |
|---:|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1** | *"an area of over **80 million hectares** or about one-fourth of our total area is exposed to wind and water erosion"* | Khullar, local scan PDF p.120, folio 207, *Extent of Soil Erosion in India* | Wind **and** water erosion, all-India | ❗ None stated | — (it is the umbrella figure) |
| **2** | *"out of which **40 million hectares** of land has undergone serious erosion"* | Same block, same sentence | Wind **and** water, all-India, *"serious"* | ❗ None stated; *"serious"* undefined | — |
| **3** | *"about **40,000 hectares** of our land is permanently lost to cultivation"* | Same block, attributed to *"Experts"* | All-India, per year by implication | ❗ *"Experts"* unnamed; no date | — |
| **4** | *"About **21 million hectares** are subject to severe wind erosion in **Rajasthan and adjoining areas of Punjab, Haryana, and Gujarat** (Fig. 7.2)."* | Khullar, folio 207, **first** statement | Wind only; **four** states | ❗ None stated; *"severe"* undefined | ❗ **Row 5, on the same page** |
| **5** | *"About **45 million hectares** of land is subject to severe wind erosion in **Rajasthan and adjoining areas of Punjab, Haryana, Gujarat and Western Uttar Pradesh** (Fig. 7.2)."* | Khullar, folio 207, **second** statement, in the next paragraph | Wind only; **five** areas — ❗ adds **Western Uttar Pradesh** | ❗ None stated; *"severe"* undefined | ❗ **Row 4, on the same page** |
| **6** | *"It is estimated that **34 lakh tonnes** of fertile soils is removed by wind every year in the districts of **Jodhpur, Bikaner, Kota, Jaipur, Bharatpur, Kishangarh** etc."* | Khullar, folio 207 | Six named districts, per year | ❗ *"It is estimated"*; no agency, no method, no date | — |
| **7** | *"about **8 million tonnes** of soil has been removed from every square kilometre area in desert during the last hundred years"* | Khullar, folio 207, *"According to the latest estimates"* | Per square kilometre, per century | ❗ No agency, no method, no date; ⚠️ dimensionally extraordinary | ⚠️ Implies a depth of removal that no other figure here supports |
| **8** | *"about **12.13 per cent** of India's total area is classed as arid and about **30 per cent** area is termed as semi-arid"* | Khullar, local scan PDF p.123, folio 212, prose | All-India shares | ❗ None stated | ❗ **Row 9, on the facing folio** |
| **9** | **Total average: arid 12.13, semi-arid 29.13 (per cent)** | Khullar, **Table 7.3**, folio 210 | All-India shares | ❗ No source line captured on the crop | ❗ **Row 8** — the prose says *"about 30"*, the table says **29.13** |
| **10** | *"the land degradation and desertification in the country has been estimated to be **97.84 million hectares in 2018-19**"* | 📰 **CA-2**, Press Information Bureau release **1946401**, Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change, **07 Aug 2023** | All-India, degradation **and** desertification | ✅ **Agency: Space Applications Centre, ISRO, Ahmedabad. Period: 2018-19. Reported by: the Ministry, in a dated release.** | — ❗ **This is the only row in the table that carries agency, method-bearer and period** |
| **11** | **~29.7 per cent / ~97.85 million hectares** for 2018-19, up from **28.76 per cent** in 2003-05 | Advanced canonical owner §1 | All-India | ⚠️ Not obtained first-party this pass; the atlas publication was **not downloaded** | ⚠️ **Row 10** — 97.85 against **97.84**; see the reconciliation below |

❗ **The table above is the only pure table used as a primary visual anywhere in this edition.** It exists because this lesson's content *is* an audit, and an audit's natural form is a ledger.

### 1. ❌ Correction box — the 21-against-45 contradiction, reproduced and never harmonised

> ❗ **Two sentences, one page, one book, two incompatible figures.** They are reproduced here exactly, in the order they appear, with nothing in between removed and nothing reconciled.
>
> ✅ *"…About **21 million hectares** are subject to severe wind erosion in Rajasthan and adjoining areas of Punjab, Haryana, and Gujarat (Fig. 7.2)."*
>
> ✅ *"Wind erosion is a serious problem in arid and semi-arid parts of north west India. About **45 million hectares** of land is subject to severe wind erosion in Rajasthan and adjoining areas of Punjab, Haryana, Gujarat and Western Uttar Pradesh (Fig. 7.2)."*
>
> ⚠️ **What differs between them.** ❗ The **scope list**: the second adds **Western Uttar Pradesh**. ⚠️ ❗ **Nothing else differs.** Both say *"severe wind erosion"*, both cite the **same figure, Fig. 7.2**, and both are on the **same printed folio**.
>
> ❗ **Why the scope difference cannot explain the gap.** ⚠️ The figure **more than doubles**, from 21 to 45 million hectares. ❗ Western Uttar Pradesh is a **humid-to-sub-humid plain, not an arid tract**, and the second sentence's own opening locates the problem *"in arid and semi-arid parts of north west India"*. ⚠️ For the addition of one sub-regional tract to add **24 million hectares** of *severe wind erosion* would require that tract to carry more severely wind-eroded land than Rajasthan, Punjab, Haryana and Gujarat combined. ❗ **That is not credible, and this edition does not propose it as a reconciliation.**
>
> ❗ **This edition does not harmonise the two figures, does not average them, does not select one, and does not silently drop either.** ⚠️ Both are reported, with their page, their scope list and their shared figure reference. ❗ **The contradiction is the lesson.**
>
> ⚠️ **The method point, which is the transferable deliverable.** ❗ **A hectare figure is not evidence unless it carries four things**: a **definition** of the class being counted — here, what *"severe"* means in measurable terms; a **spatial scope** stated explicitly; a **method** by which the area was determined; and a **date or period** of measurement. ❗ Rows 1 to 9 of the ledger carry **none of the four**. ❗ Row 10 carries **three** — agency, period and reporting instrument — and is therefore the only Indian national figure this file quotes as a figure.
>
> ⚠️ **What a candidate should actually write.** ❗ Not *"45 million hectares of India suffers severe wind erosion"*. ⚠️ Instead: *"Textbook estimates of severely wind-eroded area in north-west India vary widely — one standard source gives both 21 and 45 million hectares on a single page with differing state lists and no stated method — which is why an answer should rest on India's mapped national assessment, reported by the Ministry as 97.84 million hectares of land degradation and desertification for 2018-19 on the Space Applications Centre's authority."* ❗ **That sentence demonstrates source criticism, and source criticism is what separates a nine from a six on a *discuss* question.**

### 2. ❌ Correction box — 97.85 against 97.84, and which one controls

> ✅ **The advanced canonical owner** states that *"~29.7% of India (~97.85 mha) is degraded (2018-19), up from 28.76% (2003-05) — a rising trend."*
>
> 📰 ✅ **The first-party release** — Press Information Bureau 1946401 of **07 August 2023** — states **97.84 million hectares in 2018-19**.
>
> ❗ **The first-party figure controls: 97.84 million hectares.** ⚠️ It is quoted with its agency (the **Space Applications Centre, ISRO, Ahmedabad**), its mapped period (**2018-19**) and its reporting instrument (**the Ministry's release of 07 August 2023**).
>
> ❗ **These are not two findings.** ⚠️ They are the same assessment reported with a rounding difference, and presenting them as two measurements would be a fabrication. ❗ **This file does not present 97.85 and 97.84 as two findings.**
>
> ⚠️ **The owner's percentages are retained with an explicit limitation.** ❗ The figures **~29.7 per cent for 2018-19** and **28.76 per cent for 2003-05** were **not obtained first-party this pass**, and 📰 the Ministry's **earlier** release of **17 June 2021** announcing the atlas **states no national hectare or percentage figure at all**. ❗ The 94.8 megabyte atlas publication was **not downloaded**, so **no figure is attributed to the atlas document directly** anywhere in this file.
>
> ⚠️ **The 'rising trend' phrase needs the same care.** 📰 ✅ The 2021 release describes the atlas as providing *"the change analysis for the duration of 15 years, from 2003-05 to 2018-19."* ❗ A **two-epoch comparison** is not a time series: it cannot be converted into an annual rate, it cannot establish the shape of change between the epochs, and it cannot by itself separate a real change in land condition from a change in mapping method or classification between the two assessments. ❗ **No annual rate of degradation is stated anywhere in this file.**

### 3. ❌ Correction box — 29.13 against "about 30", on facing folios

> ✅ **Khullar's prose, folio 212:** *"about 12.13 per cent of India's total area is classed as arid and about 30 per cent area is termed as semi-arid."*
>
> ✅ **Khullar's Table 7.3, folio 210** — the facing folio — gives the **Total average** as **arid 12.13** and **semi-arid 29.13** per cent.
>
> ⚠️ **The discrepancy is small and the habit it teaches is not.** ❗ The arid figure matches exactly; the semi-arid figure has been **rounded upward in the prose**. ❗ **Quote the table value with its table number — 29.13 per cent, Table 7.3 — and note the rounding in the prose.**
>
> ⚠️ **Why a 0.87-point difference is worth a correction box.** ❗ Because it is the **same error class** as rows 4 and 5, at a scale where it is harmless. ⚠️ A reader who notices it on facing pages has installed the habit that catches the 21-against-45 contradiction two folios earlier. ❗ **Precision is a practice, not an event.**

### 4. The state-resolved evidence that does survive the audit

⚠️ Three of Khullar's tables are **internally consistent and state-resolved**, and they are the most useful Indian material in this topic — provided each is quoted **with its table number** and **as a source-era book figure with no stated agency or date**.

**✅ Table 7.3 — Area under arid and semi-arid conditions in India, as a percentage of total geographical area** (transcribed in full from 400 dots-per-inch crops):

| State | Arid | Semi-arid |
|---|---:|---:|
| Rajasthan | 57.42 | 36.67 |
| Gujarat | 33.72 | 47.50 |
| Haryana | 29.32 | 59.77 |
| Punjab | 24.60 | 75.40 |
| Maharashtra | 0.42 | 61.17 |
| Andhra Pradesh and Telangana | 7.18 | 44.66 |
| Karnataka | 4.27 | 72.60 |
| Jammu and Kashmir | 31.13 | 6.22 |
| Uttar Pradesh | — | 21.73 |
| Madhya Pradesh | — | 13.38 |
| Tamil Nadu | — | 15.54 |
| **Total average** | **12.13** | **29.13** |

❗ **Four readings worth taking from Table 7.3.** ⚠️ **Rajasthan alone is 57.42 per cent arid**, which corroborates Khullar's prose that *"more than half of the total area"* of Rajasthan is arid. ⚠️ **Punjab is 75.40 per cent semi-arid and Karnataka 72.60 per cent**, which means the semi-arid class is **not a north-western phenomenon** — it is national. ⚠️ **Jammu and Kashmir is 31.13 per cent arid with only 6.22 per cent semi-arid**, a profile unlike any other state, and the reason is the **cold desert**, not the hot one. ❗ **Maharashtra is 0.42 per cent arid and 61.17 per cent semi-arid**, which is the sharpest illustration that *arid* and *semi-arid* are different classes with different distributions and must not be summed casually.

**✅ Table 7.1 — Area under ravines in India (lakh hectares):**

| Rajasthan | 4.52 |

**✅ Table 7.2 — Salinity affected areas in India (lakh hectares):**

| | | | Tamil Nadu | 0.04 |

❗ **The decisive comparison this edition makes with these three tables, and it is the owned demand's limiting argument in Indian numbers.** ⚠️ Set the ravine table and the salinity table beside the wind-erosion claims. ❗ **Uttar Pradesh leads both the ravine table (12.30 lakh hectares) and the salinity table (12.95 lakh hectares), and Rajasthan leads neither.** ⚠️ ❗ **Water erosion and salinity are not minor adjuncts to a wind-erosion story; they are large, state-resolved, independently tabulated degradation pathways, and in the sources' own numbers they are concentrated in different states from the wind pathway.** ❗ That single observation is what prevents the owned answer from collapsing into *"desertification equals wind"*.

### 5. The map evidence, and what a legend can establish

✅ **Fig. 7.2, "India: Soil Erosion"**, at local scan PDF p.119, is the decisive Topic-07 map in the Indian material, and its **legend is the evidence**, not its outline. ✅ The legend carries, as separate classes: **gully and sheet erosion**; **sheet erosion**; ❗ **desert erosion (aeolian)**; **erosion due to shifting cultivation**; **glacial erosion**; and **coastal erosion** — together with a dashed line labelled ❗ **"LIMIT OF WIND EROSION"**.

⚠️ **Three things that legend establishes without any figure at all.** ❗ First, the source's own cartography treats **aeolian erosion as one class among six**, which is the limiting argument in cartographic form. ❗ Second, the existence of a drawn **limit of wind erosion** means the author held wind erosion to have a **definable spatial extent** — which makes the absence of a method for the 21 and 45 million hectare figures all the more conspicuous. ❗ Third, ⚠️ **a limit line is not a frontier line**: it marks where a process class is mapped, not a boundary that is moving.

✅ Two further map assets are named: **Fig. 7.3 "India: Ravines"** at folio 208, and **Fig. 7.4 "India: Saline and Alkaline Lands"** at folio 210. ⚠️ Together with Fig. 7.2 they give the three degradation pathways **three separate national maps in one book** — which is, again, the limiting argument.

### 6. The mechanism in the source's own Indian words

✅ Khullar's wind-erosion paragraph at folio 206 restates in Indian terms everything built in Arc A, and the correspondence is worth making explicit because it demonstrates that this file's two arcs are one argument.

| Khullar's Indian statement | 🔁 The world-process lesson that built it |
|---|---|
| ✅ *"In arid and semi arid lands with little rainfall, the wind acts as a powerful agent of soil erosion"* | 🔁 **L1** — aridity as the enabling condition |
| ✅ *"Wind erosion is accentuated when the soil is dry, weakly aggregated, devoid of vegetation cover along with over grazing and the winds are strong"* | 🔁 **L3** — the supply ladder: dry, uncohesive, exposed |
| ✅ *"Even modest wind velocities can keep individual particles of humus, clay and silt in suspension"* | 🔁 **L4** — suspension, and ❗ note *humus* is named: the **organic fraction** is in the exported load |
| ✅ Very fine, fine and medium sands moved *"in a succession of bounds and leaps, known as saltation"*; coarse sand *"rolled along the soil surface… called surface creep"* | 🔁 **L4** — the three sorted modes |
| ✅ Coarse sand *"(1.0-2.0 mm in diameter), gravels, peds and clods are too large to be rolled by wind"*, leaving surfaces *"covered with coarse fragments larger than 1.00 mm in diameter. This kind of arid soil surface is known as desert pavement"* | 🔁 **L5 and L9** — the lag and the armouring feedback |
| ✅ Human factors: **deforestation, overgrazing, faulty methods of agriculture, shifting cultivation** | 🔁 **L3** — lowering rungs two and three without changing the climate |

✅ Khullar also supplies the **tempo** argument in Indian terms, which 🔁 **Lesson 8** built for the world case: *"It is not the total annual rainfall that is important, but rather how and when it comes. A single heavy downpour continuing for a few hours may result in severe soil erosion, while the same amount of rainfall fairly distributed over a longer period may cause little erosion."*

✅ And the ravine sequence, with its dimensions: rills deepen into **gullies**, and where a gully bed *"is turned into an immediate drop of 3 to 4 metres and gradually flattens out, a ravine is formed. The depth of a ravine may extend to 30 metres or even more."* 🔁 Ravine **genesis** belongs to Topic 05; ❗ here the ravine is a **degradation class with a national table and a national map**, and nothing more.

### 7. 🇮🇳 India example and its limitation — what the national assessment actually is

📰 ✅ **CA-2's second clause is the one most often overlooked and it is the most useful.** The release records that *"An online portal has been developed with the help of Space Application Center (SAC), Ahmedabad for visualization of degraded area of land with the processes causing degradation."*

❗ **Read that carefully: the national system maps degraded area *with the processes causing degradation*.** ⚠️ ❗ That is a **process-attributed, class-based, spatially explicit assessment** — exactly the opposite of a single hectare figure with no method. ⚠️ 📰 ✅ **CA-5** confirms it is a **programme rather than a one-off**: the Space Applications Centre's own index lists a **Land Degradation Vulnerability Atlas**, the **2021** atlas, the **2018** atlas in two volumes, a **Desertification Status Mapping Technical Guideline** and a **2016** atlas, with a separate **dashboard** under applications. ❗ **The existence of a published technical guideline is itself the methodological answer to rows 1 to 9 of the ledger.**

❗ **The limitations, five of them, each material.**

- ❗ **The atlas publication was not downloaded** (94.8 megabytes). ⚠️ Its **existence, publisher, edition list and mapped period** are established first-party by the Ministry's 2021 release and by the Centre's own index; ❗ **no figure is attributed to the atlas document itself anywhere in this file.**
- ❗ **The Centre's index page is undated on its face.** ⚠️ It is cited as *"the Space Applications Centre VEDAS Desertification Status Mapping Atlas index, fetched during the preparation of this edition"*, and the **2021 edition is named explicitly** rather than described as "the latest".
- ❗ **No state-wise degradation figure was obtained first-party.** ⚠️ The advanced owner's **ranking** — that Rajasthan carries the largest desertified area — and its **aeolian-share statement** — that wind erosion drives more than 40 per cent of Rajasthan's desertification — rest on that owner alone and are used **with the owner named and the period given**. ❗ **No percentage is attached to Rajasthan's total anywhere in this file.**
- ❗ **The figure 97.84 million hectares covers *land degradation and desertification* together**, in the release's own words. ⚠️ It is **not** a wind-erosion figure, **not** a Thar figure and **not** a desert-area figure. ❗ Using it as any of those would be a category error.
- ⚠️ **The Indian Council of Forestry Research and Education Centre of Excellence** named in the same release, and the **Bonn Challenge reporting assignment to the International Union for Conservation of Nature, India**, are institutional facts; ❗ they are named at Lesson 16 and are **not** evidence of measured land condition.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| Evidence class | Example from this lesson | Can it support a claim about extent? | Can it support a claim about change? |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Undated textbook hectare figure** | Rows 1 to 7 | ❗ **No** — no definition, scope, method or date | ❗ **No** |
| **Internally consistent textbook table, quoted with its number** | Tables 7.1, 7.2, 7.3 | ⚠️ **Partly** — as a **source-era relative pattern** across states | ❗ **No** — single snapshot, no date |
| **Map legend with named process classes** | Fig. 7.2 | ⚠️ **Qualitatively** — it establishes that aeolian is one class of six | ❗ **No** |
| **Dated first-party national figure with agency and period** | Row 10, CA-2 | ✅ **Yes**, as a **mapped condition for 2018-19** | ⚠️ **Only** via the two-epoch comparison, which is not a rate |
| **Two-epoch comparison** | 2003-05 against 2018-19, per CA-1 | — | ⚠️ **Direction only**, and with a method-change caveat; ❗ **never an annual rate** |

**Strongest objection.** A lesson devoted to showing that the figures are unusable leaves a candidate with nothing to write in an examination hall.

**Reply.** It leaves the candidate with **one usable national figure, three usable state tables, three national maps and a method** — which is considerably more than the alternative of memorising a number that a careful examiner may know to be contested. ❗ More importantly, the owned demand asks candidates to **discuss**, and a discussion that says *"the Indian evidence on wind erosion extent is inconsistent across standard sources, which is why the mapped national assessment reported by the Ministry for 2018-19 is the proper anchor"* is **visibly stronger** than one that asserts 45 million hectares. ⚠️ The audit also produces the limiting argument directly: ❗ the same book that gives the wind figures gives **separate national tables and separate national maps for ravines and for salinity**, with **Uttar Pradesh leading both** — which is the best available evidence that aeolian action is **one pathway among several**.

⚠️ **Residual.** ❗ **This edition cannot determine which of Khullar's two wind-erosion figures, if either, is correct**, because neither carries a method. ⚠️ Nor can it determine **how much of India's 97.84 million hectares is attributable to aeolian processes**, because the process-attributed breakdown lives in a publication that was not obtained. ❗ **No aeolian share of India's degraded area is stated anywhere in this file**, and that absence is itself reported at Lesson 18 as a limit on how precise the owned answer can be.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** *"Geographical features and their location-changes… and the effects of such changes"*, General Studies Paper I. 🔁 Convention and policy routed to the environment-and-ecology owner; conservation agronomy routed to the agriculture owner.
- **Verified linkage:** ✅ **2026 General Studies Paper I, Question 6.** This lesson builds the **land-productivity effects** element and supplies the **evidence base** for the **human amplification** element; it is also where the **limiting argument** acquires Indian numbers. ⚠️ 🔁 **A4 — 2018 Prelims, Question 82**, on agricultural soil organic matter, the sulphur cycle and salinisation, is routed to the **environment-and-ecology owner** and is named here **as a demand hook only**; ❗ its official key is unavailable locally and **no answer is recorded or inferred**.
- **Traps:** quoting 45 million hectares as India's wind-eroded area; quoting 21 million hectares as if the other sentence did not exist; averaging or reconciling the two; treating 97.84 million hectares as a desert area, a Thar area or a wind-erosion figure; converting the two-epoch comparison into an annual rate; attributing any figure to the atlas publication; writing *"about 30 per cent"* when the table says 29.13; repeating the per-square-kilometre century figure as fact.
- **Answer use:** the audit sentence is a distinctive opening for any India degradation question. *"India's standard textbook figures for severely wind-eroded area are internally inconsistent — one source gives both 21 and 45 million hectares on a single page with differing state lists and no stated method — so the defensible anchor is the Ministry's reported figure of 97.84 million hectares of land degradation and desertification for 2018-19, on the Space Applications Centre's mapping, which is a condition for a stated period rather than a rate."*

Revision notes:

- ❗ **[N3] The contradiction, both halves, never harmonised:** ✅ *"About **21 million hectares** are subject to severe wind erosion in Rajasthan and adjoining areas of **Punjab, Haryana, and Gujarat**"*; ✅ *"About **45 million hectares** of land is subject to severe wind erosion in Rajasthan and adjoining areas of **Punjab, Haryana, Gujarat and Western Uttar Pradesh**."* ❗ Same folio, same figure reference, both *"severe"*, no method on either.
- ❗ **The scope difference cannot explain a doubling.** Western Uttar Pradesh would have to exceed four arid states combined. ❗ **Not proposed as a reconciliation.**
- ❗ **The four-part test for any hectare figure:** **definition** of the class · **spatial scope** · **method** · **date or period**. ❗ Rows 1-9 carry none; row 10 carries three.
- ✅ **Umbrella figures (Khullar, folio 207):** *"over 80 million hectares or about one-fourth of our total area is exposed to wind and water erosion"*; *"40 million hectares… has undergone serious erosion"*; *"about 40,000 hectares… permanently lost to cultivation"*; *"34 lakh tonnes of fertile soils is removed by wind every year"* in **Jodhpur, Bikaner, Kota, Jaipur, Bharatpur, Kishangarh**.
- ❗ **[U10] Not quoted as fact:** *"about 8 million tonnes of soil has been removed from every square kilometre area in desert during the last hundred years"* — no agency, no method, dimensionally extraordinary. ⚠️ Used once, attributed, as a specimen a careful answer declines to use.
- ❗ **[N9] 97.84 controls.** 📰 ✅ PIB 1946401, **07 Aug 2023**: *"97.84 million hectares in 2018-19"*, on the **SAC-ISRO** atlas's authority. ⚠️ The advanced owner's **97.85** is the **same assessment rounded differently** — ❗ **not two findings**. ⚠️ Owner's **29.7 %** and **28.76 %** retained **only** with the note that they were **not obtained first-party**; 📰 the **17 Jun 2021** release states **no national figure at all**; the atlas publication was **not downloaded** · ❗ **Mapped condition ≠ trend.** The **2003-05 → 2018-19** change analysis is a **two-epoch comparison**. ❗ **No annual rate is stated anywhere in this file.**
- ❗ **[N12] 29.13 against "about 30":** quote **Table 7.3's 29.13 %** with its table number; note the prose rounding. ⚠️ Arid **12.13 %** matches in both.
- ✅ **Table 7.3 readings:** **Rajasthan 57.42 arid**; **Punjab 75.40 semi-arid**, **Karnataka 72.60** — ❗ the semi-arid class is **national, not north-western**; **Jammu and Kashmir 31.13 arid / 6.22 semi-arid** — the **cold desert** profile; **Maharashtra 0.42 arid / 61.17 semi-arid** — ❗ the two classes are **not interchangeable**.
- ✅ **Table 7.1 ravines (lakh ha):** UP **12.30** · MP **6.83** · Rajasthan **4.52** · Gujarat **4.00** · Punjab **1.20** · West Bengal **1.04** · Bihar **0.60** · Tamil Nadu **0.60** · Maharashtra **0.20** · ✅ **Table 7.2 salinity (lakh ha):** UP **12.95** · Punjab **12.25** · Gujarat **12.14** · West Bengal **8.50** · Rajasthan **7.28** · Maharashtra **5.34** · Haryana **5.26** · Karnataka **4.04** · Odisha **4.04** · MP **2.24** · AP and Telangana **0.42** · Delhi **0.11** · Bihar **0.04** · Tamil Nadu **0.04**.
- ❗ **The limiting argument in Indian numbers:** **Uttar Pradesh leads BOTH the ravine and the salinity table; Rajasthan leads neither.** ❗ Wind is **one pathway among several**.
- ✅ **Fig. 7.2 legend:** gully and sheet erosion · sheet erosion · ❗ **desert erosion (aeolian)** · shifting-cultivation erosion · glacial erosion · coastal erosion, plus a dashed ❗ **"LIMIT OF WIND EROSION"**. ⚠️ **Six classes, one of them aeolian.** ❗ A limit line is **not a frontier line**. ✅ Also **Fig. 7.3 Ravines** and **Fig. 7.4 Saline and Alkaline Lands**.
- ✅ **Tempo (Khullar):** *"It is not the total annual rainfall that is important, but rather how and when it comes."* ✅ **Ravine depth** *"may extend to 30 metres or even more"*, from a drop of **3 to 4 metres**.
- 📰 ✅ **CA-2's portal clause:** degraded area visualised *"with the processes causing degradation"* — ❗ a **process-attributed, class-based** assessment. 📰 ✅ **CA-5:** the atlas is a **series** — Vulnerability Atlas, **2021**, **2018** Vols 1-2, a **Technical Guideline**, **2016**, plus a dashboard. ❗ The guideline's existence is the methodological answer to rows 1-9.
- ❗ **Not stated anywhere in this file:** an aeolian share of India's degraded area · a state-wise degradation percentage · an annual degradation rate · any figure attributed to the atlas publication itself.
- 🔁 **A4 (2018 Prelims Q82)** named as a demand hook only; owned by the environment-and-ecology file. ❗ **No key recorded or inferred.**

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate's notes contain the line: "45 million hectares of India is under severe wind erosion (Khullar)." Audit that line, and write a replacement that would survive examiner scrutiny.

**Model answer:** The line fails on four counts and misreports its own source on a fifth. First, **the source does not say that**: it says 45 million hectares *in Rajasthan and adjoining areas of Punjab, Haryana, Gujarat and Western Uttar Pradesh*, which is a regional scope, not an all-India one. Second, **the same page also says 21 million hectares**, for the same class of *severe wind erosion*, citing the same figure, with the state list differing only by the addition of Western Uttar Pradesh — and the addition of one sub-regional tract cannot more than double a severity class that the sentence itself locates in arid and semi-arid north-west India. Third, **"severe" is undefined**: no threshold of soil loss, depth, cover or productivity decline is stated, so the class being counted is unknown. Fourth, **no method, agency or date accompanies either figure**, so the number cannot be checked, updated or compared with anything. Fifth, quoting one of two contradictory figures from the same page without acknowledging the other is a **selection**, and an examiner who knows the source will read it as one. A replacement would be: *"Standard textbook estimates of severely wind-eroded area in north-west India are internally inconsistent — Khullar gives both 21 and 45 million hectares on a single folio, with differing state lists and no stated method — so the defensible national anchor is the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change's reported figure of 97.84 million hectares of land degradation and desertification for 2018-19, mapped by the Space Applications Centre, which is a condition for a stated period rather than a rate, and which covers all degradation processes rather than wind alone."*

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat a published number as evidence merely because it is published and attributed to a standard author. A figure becomes evidence when it carries a definition of what is counted, a stated spatial scope, a method and a period; without those it is an assertion with a citation attached.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "In assessing land degradation, the quality of a figure matters more than its size." Discuss with Indian examples. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The proposition is well supported by India's evidence base. A standard textbook gives, on a single folio, both 21 and 45 million hectares as the area under severe wind erosion in north-west India, with state lists differing only by Western Uttar Pradesh, no definition of "severe", no method and no date; the larger figure is therefore not the better one. By contrast the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change reports 97.84 million hectares of land degradation and desertification for 2018-19, mapped by the Space Applications Centre, with a published technical guideline and an online portal visualising degraded area together with the processes causing degradation. That figure carries agency, period and method-bearer, so it can be checked and compared. Its limits must also be respected: it is a mapped condition, not a trend, and the 2003-05 comparison is a two-epoch change analysis that cannot be converted into an annual rate.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the internal contradiction reproduced accurately with both figures and both scope lists; 2 for the four-part test — definition, scope, method, period — stated as the criterion; 3 for the first-party national figure quoted with agency, period and reporting instrument, and at least one methodological strength named; 2 for the mapped-condition-against-trend limitation applied to the two-epoch comparison. Deduct 3 for quoting either wind figure as settled and 2 for presenting the national figure as a desert or wind-erosion area.

## Lesson 15 — Desertification defined: desert, aridity, drought, degradation

Progress: L15 / 18 — Desertification defined: desert, aridity, drought, degradation  |  Stage: Applied  |  Band: Heaviest

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Canonical basis: Basic owner §8.4 in full — *"A **desert** is a climatic zone. **Desertification** is land degradation in arid, semi-arid and dry sub-humid areas, driven by climatic variation and human activity. A desert cannot 'desertify'; the land at risk is the productive dryland margin around it"*; the human-driver paragraph; the reversibility point and the *"advancing desert"* correction; the factual caution. The **terminology-control clause**: *"desert differs from aridity, drought, land degradation and UNCCD desertification… restoration commitment differs from measured restoration outcome."* The **boundary clause**: *"Environment and Ecology owns the direct UNCCD/desertification policy PYQ."*
Book basis: D.R. Khullar, local scan PDF p.123, folio 212, for the book definition of desertification and the seven ecological implications; local scan PDF p.135, folio 237, for the **drought** definition and the High Powered Committee on Disaster Management formulation — the only first-party textbook definition available that separates a temporary event from a permanent state.
CA: ✅ **CA-1** — Press Information Bureau release **1727987**, **17 June 2021**, on the release of the atlas, which states it is *"published by Space Application Centre, ISRO, Ahmedabad"*, *"provides state wise area of degraded lands for the time frame 2018-19"* and also *"the change analysis for the duration of 15 years, from 2003-05 to 2018-19"*; released on **Desertification and Drought Day**; records that India **hosted UNCCD COP 14 in September 2019** and is striving toward **Land Degradation Neutrality and restoration of 26 million hectares of degraded land by 2030**. ❗ **This release states no national hectare or percentage figure.** ✅ **CA-2** — release **1946401**, **07 August 2023**: **97.84 million hectares in 2018-19**. ✅ **CA-5** — the Space Applications Centre VEDAS index, naming the atlas series and a **Desertification Status Mapping Technical Guideline**. ✅ **CA-6** — the United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification page for **COP16, Riyadh, 2 to 13 December 2024**, theme ***"Our Land. Our Future."***
Boundary: 🔁 **The environment-and-ecology owner owns desertification as a convention, an instrument and a policy demand**, and owns the **2020 General Studies Paper I demand** on desertification as a process without climatic boundaries. ❗ This lesson uses that demand as a **bounded cross-owner application** and stops at the point where the question becomes *"what should the convention or the state do?"* 🔁 **Disaster management** owns drought declaration, relief and response doctrine; the drought definition is taken for separation only and **no response doctrine appears**. ❗ **No Great Indian Bustard content anywhere.**
Trap: the highest-value trap in the topic — treating *desert* and *desertification* as the same word with different endings. Three of the seven adjacent demands sit on the other side of this boundary.

   |   |   |  is no productivity   |   | PRODUCTIVE MARGIN, not  |   |   |

        | normal or expected amount for a specific period              |

        |   from whatever the local normal happens to be               |
        +-------------------------------------------------------------+

   [1] DESERT is not DESERTIFICATION: a zone vs a process
   [2] ARIDITY is not DROUGHT:        a state vs an event
   [3] DEGRADATION is not DESERTIFICATION: the latter is the former
       RESTRICTED BY CLIMATE ZONE

       in place, by FOUR pathways - wind, water, vegetation, chemical
